This presentation will highlight recent developments of GENCI including Jean Zay, the more sought AI supercomputer in Europe for research, Alice Recoque the 2nd Exascale system in Europe, quantum computers and developments on sovereign AI Co Scientists and AI technologies</p>

### Multiplayer factories - leading the shift to concurrent delivery

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 11:50–12:15
- Room: Amphitheater
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Patrick Debois

<p>Code review is becoming the bottleneck in AI-assisted software development. Agents generate changes faster than humans can review them. Teams are responding with automated validation, risk-based auto-merging, and increasingly autonomous software factories.</p>
<p>But what happens when you actually turn on the factory?</p>
<p>Suddenly, hundreds of agents are working concurrently, each in its own sandbox, making changes against an evolving codebase. Pull requests compete for integration. Context becomes stale. Decisions conflict. And while agents can generate code in parallel, our delivery infrastructure still largely assumes a sequential path toward one main.</p>
<p>A new engineering discipline is emerging: concurrent delivery. Inspired by challenges familiar from concurrent programming and distributed systems, developers are exploring new approaches to coordination, isolation, integration, shared context, and orchestration. And as execution becomes cheaper, entirely new possibilities emerge: competing implementations, continuous architectural exploration, and software evolving in multiple directions simultaneously.</p>
<p>In this talk, we'll map the emerging tools, architectural patterns, and unsolved problems of concurrent delivery. From speculative merging and agent swarms to shared memory, decision reconciliation, and autonomous factory operations, we'll explore what developers are building today—and what becomes possible next.</p>
<p>Because the next challenge isn't making coding agents faster. It's figuring out how to deliver when they all work at once.</p>

### What Did the Agent Know, and When? Auditable Memory and API Guardrails for Regulated AI

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 11:50–12:15
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API observabilty and testing for Agents
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Abhijit Dey

<p>Rule-based systems were auditable by default. You could read the code and know exactly what happened. AI agents break that assumption — they reason, adapt, and carry context forward in ways nobody wrote down in advance. Which leaves regulated industries with an uncomfortable question: when an examiner asks why a customer was declined last March, can you actually answer?
This talk argues that two architectural layers make agents governable, and neither of them is the model.</p>
<p>APIs are the action guardrail — the deterministic boundary between what an agent decides and what actually happens to a customer's money. The agent is allowed to think. The APIs define what it's allowed to do.</p>
<p>Memory is the evidence guardrail — and it's the one almost everyone skips. An agent's decision is a product of what it knew at that moment: retrieved context, prior interactions, the policy version in force. Memory that isn't versioned, scoped, and reconstructable is a compliance liability wearing a productivity costume.</p>
<p>You'll leave with a concrete architecture pattern, a reconstruction test for your own systems, and the questions to ask before your first agent reaches production.</p>

### You Cannot Bolt Agent Readiness Onto Ungoverned APIs

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 11:50–12:15
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: The Future of Software Technology
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kin Lane

<p>&quot;AI-ready&quot; is now claimed by every API program and measured by almost none. API Evangelist scores roughly 25,000 providers on two separate instruments: the Kin Score, a 0–100 composite of governance fundamentals with a conditional regulatory layer for regulated industries, and Agent Readiness, a standalone 0–100 rating across twelve dimensions. They are computed independently and never blended — which means the relationship between them is a finding, not an assumption.</p>
<p>The finding is that agent readiness is a layer built on the governance foundation, and it does not rise above it. An agent does not consume an API; it tries to complete a business capability — open the account, file the claim, move the shipment, settle the payment — and it fails at whichever contract in that chain is ungoverned. Readiness is therefore a property of a capability path, not of an endpoint, and the weakest governed link sets the ceiling for the whole path. This is why organisations that map their capabilities first and score their contracts second get a usable answer, and why organisations that ship an MCP server first get a confident interface to an unreliable contract.</p>
<p>My talk works down from business capability to capability path to individual contract, showing which governance facets carry the weight at each level, and what the scored catalog says about how far most API portfolios actually are. As API governance moves from an offensive strategy to a defensive one in a market being flooded with agentic activity, the API foundation you are building on will matter when it comes to adapting to the unpredictable and chaotic market we are all trying to make sense of today.</p>

<p>I'll walk through what it actually takes: working within AWS Bedrock with sovereign data isolation requirements, spinning up sandboxed containers on AWS ECS in under 20 milliseconds, designing and optimizing agent Skills for code-level API refactoring, and integrating MCP servers for GitHub and Treblle to give agents the context they need to act intelligently.</p>

### Composable Architecture for AI Systems: APIs, MCP, Skills, Sub-Agents, and Workflows

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 12:20–12:45
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Erik Wilde

<p>AI is a powerful tool, but it is not the only tool, and most systems worth building are hybrids of AI and non-AI parts. That line between them doesn't sit neatly between domains. It runs inside every domain, and it should move as you learn. This talk looks at the mechanisms for connecting across that line: APIs, MCP, agent skills, and sub-agents, as well as at workflows as a way to move the line itself: when an agent keeps rediscovering the same business process, that process belongs in deterministic execution, not in a prompt. You'll leave knowing which mechanism belongs where, and how to keep shifting work out of AI as your system settles.</p>

### Designing for developers who've changed how they build: lessons from Azure Developer CLI data

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 12:20–12:45
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: The Future of Software Technology

<p>The way developers interact with APIs has changed in a way that's not going back. Not because developers are gone (they're not) but because their relationship to your API is increasingly mediated by a coding agent. And DX has to catch up to that reality.</p>
<p>This talk maps two new developer personas that are already in your telemetry, whether you've named them or not: the developer building an agent that consumes APIs autonomously, and the developer building anything, assisted by coding agents making calls on their behalf.</p>
<p>Drawing on growth data from the Azure Developer CLI and experience leading DX for some of the world's largest API platforms, this is a practitioner's view of what these two personas need today, and what breaks when we design for neither of them.</p>

### Events as Contracts, Events as Data: Our AsyncAPI Journey

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 12:20–12:45
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Ignacio Castillejos

<p>Migrating an event-driven architecture isn't just a change of broker. It means renegotiating contracts between every producer and consumer. In this talk, we share our journey from a legacy EDA built on GCP Pub/Sub and Avro to one built on AWS EventBridge, where every event follows the CloudEvents standard and the entire event API is defined by an AsyncAPI spec. We'll also show how those same events now power our data products.</p>
<p>We'll cover:</p>
<p>Why we changed. Routing messages on our legacy stack was hard, the setup carried too much infrastructure to maintain, and Avro depended on a schema registry we didn't control.
Contract-first events with AsyncAPI. We govern our event schemas contract-first, the same way we govern our REST APIs with OpenAPI, with the spec as the source of truth. We'll show how that replaced the external schema registry and what the workflow looks like day to day.
From events to data products. Because every event has a well-defined contract, the same events that drive our services land in our data lakehouse, where they're transformed into data marts and continuously monitored for quality.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with practical lessons on using AsyncAPI to make events a reliable foundation for both services and data products.</p>

### From Motion Analysis to Agentic Video Understanding

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 12:20–12:45
- Room: Acacia 1/2
- Conference: GenerationAI
- Track: Vision & Multimodal AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Yannis Tevissen

<p>Video is the largest and least structured kind of data on the internet. This talk traces how machines learned to watch: from optical flow and early action recognition, through CNN-based video classification, to today's vision-language models that can describe long videos and answer questions about them. It then looks at what comes next, when video understanding stops being a single model call and becomes an agent. That agent chooses which frames to look at, combines vision, speech and text, and searches across whole video libraries to answer a question. Drawing on research at Moments Lab, the talk covers where current models still fall short (temporal awareness, long videos, compute cost, bias) and what it takes to build reliable agentic video systems in production.</p>

<p>Digital Sovereignty is on every lips, however whilst the tech is here, we have yet to find a financially sustainable solution to it. The tragedy of the commons of open source is not new, but we’ve got to the point of needing to solve it if we really want organisations to be sovereign and resilient and use secure and polished products.</p>
<p>With a vibrant ecosystem of vendors and developers, and having become the default choice for European Public Sector organisations looking for digitally sovereign alternatives to Teams or WhatsApp, Matrix is the poster child of Europe’s transition to secure and digitally sovereign communications. Why did it work and why will it last? But more importantly what challenges did it highlight on how Europe needs to evolve to reach its goal of resilience and sovereignty? And what does it mean for private sector organisations?</p>

### Your company doesn’t need another AI tool. It needs an operating system

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:20
- Room: Acacia 1/2
- Conference: Build It Summit Paris 2026
- Type: Keynote
- Speakers: Lena Shakurova

<p>Adding AI to individual tasks does not automatically make a company work better. Without shared context, clear ownership, and connected workflows, teams can end up with more tools and more coordination overhead. An AI operating system is a structured way of running a business where people and AI agents work together across connected processes. In this session, we’ll cover: - The essential layers of an AI operating system: shared context, tools and connectors, safety controls and the AI operating layer. - How people and AI agents work together, with clear ownership, approvals, and escalation paths. - How to start with one useful workflow and expand without unnecessary complexity. Attendees will leave with a blueprint for connecting their scattered AI automations into a coherent operating model.</p>

### AI Agents Evaluation with synthetic data

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Agent Security
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Louis Jourdain

<p>As enterprises transition from reactive chatbots to proactive ReAct-based agents, a critical bottleneck has emerged: the Reliability Gap. Current benchmarks show that while top-tier models excel at single-turn reasoning, their success rate plummets by up to 75% in long-horizon, multi-step workflows. We can no longer rely on static test suites or manual &quot;&quot;vibe checks&quot;&quot; to evaluate agents that operate in dynamic, ever-changing environments.This talk introduces a novel framework for the automated generation of synthetic, state-aware evaluation datasets. Moving beyond simple Q&amp;A, our system utilizes a multi-agent architecture to synthesize complex dual-control scenarios, inspired by the rigor of tau bench (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07982">https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07982</a>) where both the user and the agent possess tools to modify a shared world state.</p>
<p>To deploy agents in mission-critical sectors like Telecom, Finance, or Healthcare, we need a &quot;&quot;Generic Evaluation Engine&quot;&quot; that can: -Map custom tool definitions into verifiable state-transition trees. -Generate adversarial &quot;&quot;User Personas&quot;&quot; that stress-test policy adherence and edge-case recovery. -Implement metrics to measure consistency over stochastic drift.We will demonstrate how this &quot;&quot;Mirror World&quot;&quot; approach allows teams to identify cascading failures before they hit production, turning the &quot;&quot;messy reality&quot;&quot; of agent evaluation into a structured, engineering discipline.</p>

### From Vendor Dependency to Open Source: A Journey in Trade-offs

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Sadique Akhtar, Nitya Tripathi

<p>As software organizations face increasing pressure to optimize costs without compromising reliability, scalability, or developer experience, the choice between SaaS platforms and open-source alternatives is becoming more strategic than ever.</p>
<p>In this talk, we share our journey of re-evaluating API gateway technology in response to changing cost considerations and the evolving open-source ecosystem. Starting with a SaaS-based gateway, moving to Kong OSS, and exploring Apache APISIX through a proof of concept, we will discuss the factors that shaped our decisions, the trade-offs we encountered, and the lessons learned along the way.</p>
<p>The discussion goes beyond comparing gateway products. We will explore how to balance licensing and operational costs, engineering effort, platform capabilities, community contributions, long-term maintainability, and the risks of vendor dependency. We will also examine why adopting open source is not simply about reducing licensing costs, but about understanding the total cost of ownership and building the expertise needed to operate and evolve a platform sustainably.</p>
<p>Our goal is to share a practical, experience-driven perspective on making technology choices in a cost-conscious environment—helping teams assess when open source makes sense, what trade-offs to expect, and how to make informed decisions without treating any single technology as a one-size-fits-all solution.</p>

<p>Platform Engineering 1.0 reduced developer cognitive load — but rigid architectures, a developer-only focus, and reactive security and FinOps practices are now limiting its potential. A strategic evolution is overdue.</p>
<p>This session introduces Platform Engineering 2.0: an AI-native, autonomous, and business-aligned framework built on five core pillars — Multi-Persona Experience, Embedded FinOps, Security Shift Down, Composable Architecture, and an AI-Native Platform. Learn how each pillar addresses today's demands and future-proofs your organization.</p>
<p>You'll leave with actionable recommendations for executives and platform engineers alike — including how to build a PE 2.0 business case that reframes the platform as strategic infrastructure, tied directly to developer velocity, cloud cost reduction, and AI readiness.</p>

### Securing and Industrializing AI Adoption at Scale

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 6/7
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Security for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Nejmeddine Ben Ouarred, Youssef Guenoun

<p>The adoption of AI and agents promises an efficiency revolution, but it raises a major challenge: how do we transition from an isolated prototype to a secure, industrial-grade platform? Without a control layer, opening LLMs to the information system exposes the enterprise to security risks, cost overruns, and non-compliance.</p>
<p>In this talk, GroupBees shares its vision and expertise on the architecture of agentic platforms and the emergence of AI Gateways as a core pillar of AI-driven architectures.</p>
<p>Agenda:</p>
<p>Genesis of AI Gateways: From API Management to the AI Gateway</p>
<p>Governance &amp; Security: The AI Gateway as a &quot;firewall&quot; (granular authentication, content filtering, prompt injection prevention)</p>
<p>Operational Control: Quotas, rate limiting, and traceability to manage costs and automated decisions</p>
<p>Lessons Learned &amp; Vision: Key success factors observed with our clients for deploying AI with total security</p>
<p>Target Audience: CTOs, Architects, Product Owners, and Technical Leaders looking to transform the excitement around AI into a stable, governed production platform.</p>

### Why You (too) Need an (API) Style Guide

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Design for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Robert Lehmann

<p>You’ve probably been in this situation:  You thought long and hard about this new API of yours, but now it comes to naming — shock, horror, what will you do?  Or, worse, you’re a reviewer and you’re not sure if the proposed API quite aligns with the status quo.  That’s where API Style Guides come in and can eliminate (some of the) bikeshedding.</p>
<p>In this talk, we will explore:</p>
<ul>
<li>What is an API Style Guide, and why could you possibly need one?</li>
<li>What are the characteristics of a good style guide, and what constitutes a bad one?</li>
<li>Some exemplary style guides for your to be inspired by (e.g. AIP, AEP)</li>
<li>Where else can style guides help (it’s 2026, do I need to spell it out: AI)</li>
</ul>
<p>Robert will draw on his decade+ at Google where he was one of 3% of the company allowed to perform API reviews.  His team maintained multiple style guides in order to scale reviews.</p>

### Your Portal Has Two Kinds of Visitors Now: Arazzo Workflows for Humans and Agents

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Pubudu Gunatilaka

<p>OpenAPI tells an agent which operations exist. It does not tell it that placing an order takes four calls across three APIs, in a specific order, passing an identifier from step two into step four. Agents rediscover that sequence by trial and error, and they get it wrong in ways that are expensive to debug and risky to run in production.</p>
<p>We built API Workflows into our developer portal on top of Arazzo. Each workflow pairs an Arazzo definition, which tells an agent exactly how the calls chain together, with a natural language agent prompt that tells it when reaching for this workflow is the right move. Agents turn out to need both: the mechanics and the intent.</p>
<p>The more interesting half is publication. One workflow is rendered in the portal for human developers and emitted into llms.txt and api-workflows.md for agents, with visibility controlled independently per audience and a draft-and-publish lifecycle so nothing reaches either surface early.</p>
<p>This talk covers what Arazzo gave us for free, what we had to build around it, and why publishing outcome-level workflows beats handing agents a catalog of endpoints.</p>
<p>Key Takeaways:</p>
<ul>
<li>Why OpenAPI alone leaves agents guessing at multi-step business processes, and precisely what Arazzo adds</li>
<li>Pairing a machine-readable Arazzo definition with natural-language invocation guidance, and why agents need both</li>
<li>Publishing the same workflow to human and agent surfaces, including llms.txt and markdown, from one definition</li>
<li>Governing agent-facing capability with independent per-audience visibility and a publish lifecycle</li>
<li>Where Arazzo fit cleanly, where we had to extend around it, and what that suggests for the spec's evolution</li>
</ul>

<p>AI providers rarely disclose the energy cost of their models. Estimation tools exist, but they rely on lab conditions that don't reflect real-world usage. <p>Why do digital impact estimates vary by 10x, 100x, sometimes 1000x? How come respected and responsible scientists disagree on what to do with those estimates? How to interpret them? Answer: It boils down to the subtle methodological nuances and choices underpinning sustainable impact assessment. In this talk, I summarize those choices, illustrate how they matter in practice, and show how being unaware of them can easily lead you misunderstand what the numbers mean. Also, I explain why I used to be obsessed with consequential LCA, and why I was wrong.</p>

<p>Despite our best efforts in testing software, software rollouts can still go wrong, and the process to roll back and find a fix can be painful and complex. ### Data sovereignty - We wants it but is it real ?

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Sébastien Coldefy

<p>This presentation explores the concept of data sovereignty—the idea that data is subject to the laws and governance structures of the nation where it is collected or processed. It examines the growing demand for control over data (driven by privacy concerns, regulatory compliance, and national security) while questioning its practical feasibility. Key themes likely include the tension between global data flows and localized regulations (e.g., GDPR, Schrems II), technical challenges (e.g., cloud storage, cross-border transfers), and geopolitical realities (e.g., U.S. CLOUD Act, EU-U.S. Data Privacy Framework). The talk will conclude with a critical look at whether true data sovereignty is achievable in an interconnected digital world or remains an aspirational ideal.</p>

### From Validation to Semantic Guardrails: JSON Schema as the Contract Layer for Agentic Application Development

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jean-Pierre DIjcks, Beda Hammerschmidt

<p>Agentic coding systems do more than generate code snippets. They assemble applications, call tools, wire APIs, query databases, transform data, and produce user-facing experiences.
As these systems move from assistance to action, they need more than prompt-level guardrails. They need data-connected guardrails: machine-readable contracts that describe what data means, how it may be used, where it may flow, and which policies apply.
This talk presents Project Delphi, an authoritative JSON Schema repository, and proposes a new vocabulary for extending JSON Schema with semantic and governance annotations. Project Delphi is a concrete implementation, but the larger goal is broader: to explore how the JSON Schema community defines a shared, interoperable vocabulary for data semantics, policy, and AI-era application contracts.
The core idea is simple: JSON Schema should remain the structural contract for data, while the vocabulary adds the semantic layer needed by agents, tools, and generated applications. This can describe document types, field roles, sensitivity classifications, PII/internal-only markers, lineage, retention, masking requirements, and AI usage permissions, as well as non-functional requirements.
We will explore how this changes the application development model. Instead of an agent merely generating code against loosely described APIs, it can consult authoritative schemas and use semantic annotations to make policy-aware decisions: which fields may be shown, logged, embedded, summarized, joined, exported, or passed to a tool. Applications generated or modified by agents can then project data safely and consistently according to schema-level policy, rather than relying on scattered hand-coded checks.
The session will cover a concrete technical model for schema-backed guardrails: registry-managed schemas, semantic annotations, field-level policy enforcement, safe data projection, tool-call contracts, compatibility checks, and runtime validation. We will close with open design questions and an invitation for the JSON Schema community to collaborate on DSV as a standards-oriented vocabulary rather than a vendor-specific extension.</p>

### Scaling AI-Driven engineering without scaling risk

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: The Future of APIs
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jean Burellier

<p>AI is changing how we build software. Development cycles are getting shorter, and the volume of code, services, and APIs reaching production is increasing. But as engineering teams move faster, traditional approaches to security, compliance, and governance struggle to keep up. How do we take advantage of AI-driven productivity without compromising reliability, security, or compliance?
Drawing on experience building engineering platforms in highly regulated environments and working on large-scale AI infrastructure, this talk explores how we can move away from manual approvals and release gates toward automated guardrails built directly into our development workflows.
We'll look at practical approaches to Compliance as Code, AI-assisted development, scalable CI/CD, self-service platforms, and continuous verification. We'll also discuss what happens when the bottleneck shifts from writing code to reviewing, validating, and deploying it.
The goal isn't just to ship faster. It's to make sure our engineering platforms, security practices, and compliance processes can keep up as AI changes the way we build software.</p>

### Sell the Outcome, Not the Endpoint: API-First Storytelling

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Sandeep Bhaskar

<p>Banks and financial institutions don't buy APIs — they buy outcomes: faster settlement, safer compliance, better customer experience. Yet most infrastructure vendors still pitch specs, uptime SLAs, and endpoint documentation, leaving the business case buried under technical detail. This talk makes the case for a different approach: selling API infrastructure through narrative storytelling, where every technical capability is reframed as a value story a CFO, Head of Payments, or Chief Risk Officer can act on.</p>
<p>Drawing on real presales experience helping large banks and FIs adopt API-first payment infrastructure, this session shows how to build a compelling storyline that connects API-first design principles — modularity, composability, developer experience — to concrete business value: revenue growth, risk reduction, and speed to market. We'll cover how to structure a pitch around a customer's own pain points rather than a feature list, how to use storytelling frameworks to make complex integration decisions feel low-risk, and why API-first architecture itself is a value proposition, not just an engineering choice.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with a practical framework for translating technical capability into narrative, and for pitching infrastructure the way banks actually make buying decisions — around trust, outcomes, and long-term partnership, not just technical compliance. This matters because the next wave of banking innovation depends on infrastructure teams and vendors who can speak both languages fluently.</p>

### Sovereign Technologies at Continent Scale

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Dattier 2
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Astor Nummelin Carlberg

<p>Europe doesn’t have an innovation problem in sovereign technologies. DINUM’s LaSuite runs the French state’s collaboration, Germany’s openDesk is in production across public administration, and grid operators are moving substation protection onto the open SEAPATH platform. The stacks exist and they work. What Europe has not yet built is the industrial layer that turns a successful reference deployment into millions of supported, secured and maintained ones: hardened and signed builds, lifecycle management across fleets of sites, European support teams accountable for production, and a partner channel that can deliver the same solution in every Member State. This talk asks what that layer requires and who provides it, drawing on SUSE’s experience as Europe’s largest open source company in taking community innovation into production for governments, utilities and banks.</p>

<p>In June 2026, the European Commission released the first ever EU wide Open Source Strategy (OSS), underlining the central role of open source in achieving EU technological sovereignty – defined as its capacity to develop, control and scale the technologies underpinning its economy and society, while remaining open to cooperation. Against the backdrop of the EU Tech Sovereignty Package, the Open Source Strategy will be presented and its full lifecycle approach with the ambition to grow the entire EU open source ecosystem: developing and adopting building blocks, scaling European businesses, strengthening stewardship, securing and maintaining critical software, and mobilising public administrations. We are now six months in the implementation of the Strategy. This talk will highlight some initial achievements and lay down the ongoing and future work and how the community can get involved.</p>

### You have AI. Why are your teams still copy-pasting and switching apps?

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:15
- Room: Acacia 1/2
- Conference: Build It Summit Paris 2026
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Elise Reichardt, Eva Cossart

<p>Most teams already have AI. Yet the work still stalls, because email, spreadsheets, documents and AI assistants don’t share context, and employees end up copy-pasting between them: they become the integration layer. We will share what happens when one suite connects every tool and gives access to all your company knowledge, from calendar and email to documents, financial and CRM tools, through MCP or plugins. Superhuman does this around everyday operational work, reducing manual handoffs and helping teams act without constantly switching apps.</p>

### Build your integrations for the business you're becoming - The integration platform you grow on

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: AI Gateways and Orchestration
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: John Bilay

<p>In this session, we will show you one integration platform for people and AI agents that takes you from your first flows to the one your business can’t run without. Easy to start, sturdy from day one, so growing never means rebuilding.</p>
<p>Alldotz is for teams whose integration needs grow faster than they can build. Start from a catalogue of 200+ connectors instead of a blank page, describe what you need and watch the flow appear on the canvas, so you build in a fraction of the time. Alldotz knows the systems around you: it holds their metadata and data models, so AI can generate the field mapping for you and all you do is review it. Then watch it work: simulate the flow with mock data, or dry-run it read-only against your real systems, and see every step light up as it runs. Governance keeps a to-do list of what needs your attention, from failing runs and policy breaches to deprecated functions, forgotten drafts, potential performance problems and suggested improvements to your flows and your integration landscape, so you hear about problems from Alldotz, not from your users. When a run fails, AI explains why in plain language, and self-healing proposes the fix for you to approve. And what your team builds is not just for people: expose any flow as a tool for your AI agents, so agents and humans use the same dependable integrations on one platform, with agent access protected and under your control.</p>
<p>A landscape view shows how all your systems and integrations connect, and documentation is written for you by AI, ready for your team to refine. Less time spent building, mapping and troubleshooting means faster delivery and a quicker return on your integration investment, and growing never means rebuilding.</p>

### Building an AI Model Router: Practical Lessons and Designs

- Date: 2 December 2026

### Cloud-Native Defense-in-Depth for Agentic AI: From AI Gateway to Kernel Space

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Amphitheater
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Vincent Caldeira, Christopher Nuland

<p>The transition to production-grade agentic AI introduces critical security and governance challenges due to agent autonomy. This session details a multi-tier, cloud-native architecture combining the Kubernetes AI Gateway and OpenShell to establish defense-in-depth for autonomous AI workflows.</p>
<p>At the network and payload layer, the Kubernetes AI Gateway handles protocol translation (MCP/A2A), token rate-limiting, and payload inspection. At the execution layer, OpenShell provides kernel-level container sandboxing via eBPF, seccomp, and Landlock, paired with in-process OPA policy enforcement to prevent unauthorized syscalls and data access.</p>
<p>Building on CNCF cloud-native agentic standards and a 6-layer defense in depth framework for AI agents, we demonstrate how leveraging Kubernetes RBAC, SPIFFE workload identities, and isolated agent execution planes provides a scalable, enterprise-grade control plane for agentic AI.</p>

### From Schema Registry to Agent Registry: Governing AI Discovery with the A2A Protocol

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Carles Arnal

<p>API governance solved discovery, versioning, and compatibility for REST services. Now AI agents face the same challenges. One team updates a prompt template and downstream agents silently break. Welcome to the API governance problem all over again but for agents.</p>
<p>This talk shows how Apicurio Registry (CNCF sandbox) evolved to manage AI-native artifacts using the same governance patterns that worked for APIs: A2A Agent Cards for discovery, LLM Model Schemas for metadata, and prompt templates with version control. Live demo: agents discovering each other through the A2A Protocol backed by registry-enforced compatibility rules.</p>
<p>You'll see agent cards registered via REST API, agents discover peers using the same patterns as OpenAPI discovery, and the registry reject incompatible version pushes. This brings proven API governance patterns to multi-agent AI systems.</p>
<p>Key message for API audience: if you solved API discovery and versioning, you already know how to solve agent discovery. The patterns are identical.</p>

### IT Visibility is the foundation of Digital Sovereignty

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Sylvain Melchior

<p>Can an organisation truly be sovereign if it does not fully understand its own IT landscape?</p>
<p>Digital sovereignty goes far beyond data residency or choosing European vendors. It is also about geopolitical exposure, vendor lock-in, critical dependencies, technological choices, and the ability to act when conditions change.</p>
<p>This talk explores what sovereignty concretely means for IT leaders, the difficult trade-offs between “sovereignty at any cost” and pragmatic, risk-based decisions, and why full autonomy is neither realistic nor always desirable.</p>
<p>Finally, we will examine how enterprise architecture can turn complexity into clarity, helping organisations understand dependencies, identify critical exposure, make informed trade-offs, and become more sovereign and resilient.</p>

### No Score, No MCP: Making APIs Earn Their Way Into the Agent Economy

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Design for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: André Cedik

<p>Every team wants an MCP server. Almost none of their APIs are ready for one.</p>
<p>We're a platform team responsible for 200+ APIs we don't own and built by teams that don't report to us. For years we let them deploy and manage their APIs using our platforms' UI. This led to a complicated governance mess, and we had no lever to always force good API design onto them.</p>
<p>In 2025 we've introduced a GitOps based deployment flow and things got interesting. And in 2026 agents arrived as the new consumers, and all of a sudden good API design was a necessity to make the agents understand and use the APIs.</p>
<p>So we added an AI-readiness dimension to our API quality score — deterministic, static-analysis only, every violation traceable to a line in the spec and a named rule — and we're making that score a precondition for getting an MCP server. Not a block on what you have; a gate on what you want. That changes how teams respond entirely.</p>
<p>This talk covers the mechanism and the mess: what makes an API genuinely readable by an agent rather than merely wrapped in one, how we scored that without starting an argument we'd spend a year losing, what had to be true about our deployment pipeline first, how teams actually reacted, and what we got wrong along the way.</p>

<p>Over the years, software components morphed from being developed in-house to being

include takeaways from their successes and failures, illustrated by real-life breaches that
occurred via supply chain weaknesses, including NPM, MCPs, or Docker images.</p>

### Sovereign DevOps-Driven API Governance in Practice: Open Source and Agent-Ready in Hours

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: DevSecOps for APIs and AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Andrzej Jarzyna

<p>API governance is often discussed in terms of guidelines, standards, and central API catalogues. In many organisations, though, it is still too manual, too slow, or simply arbitrary. In this talk, we will show how to make API governance part of the DevOps process. Using open source and independent tools and CI/CD pipelines, we can automatically check whether APIs follow company standards, catch breaking changes early, keep APIs catalogued, and reduce drift between guidelines and what teams actually deliver. We will also show how to put in place a bare-bones, no-fuss governance setup in just a few hours, one that brings value quickly without becoming a big governance initiative. Finally, we will show how to treat AI agents as first-class citizens, so APIs are designed to be understandable and safe to use not only for developers, but also for agent-based consumers. We will present all of this in practice in a simulated environment.</p>

### SuperStack Coalition: gathering forces towards sovereignty and scale

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:10–15:25
- Room: Dattier 2
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Niko Bonnieure

<p>The SuperStack coalition gathers 70 partners from the best open source projects in Europe, towards a Sovereign Universal Platform for European Resilience (SUPER).</p>
<p>3 millions Open Source developers in Europe, numerous SMEs, a vibrant community that gathers every year at FOSDEM, the largest developer event in the world. Still, open source solutions struggle to compete with Big Tech products and platforms. Fragmentation of our effort has been identified by many as the cause of poor adoption. End-users need a coherent and integrated offering, and so far, FOSS projects haven't been able to deliver fully. Yet, we do have almost all the parts ready, but what is missing is composing all those parts into a coherent whole.</p>
<p>Collaboration and even convergence, uniting forces across projects, finding complementarity and synergies, has been discussed for many years. Now we have formed a coalition that will support those efforts.</p>
<p>We were recently awarded a considerable grant of 29 M€ from the Open Internet Stack initiative of the European Commission DG Connect, for the next 3 years, and work has just started on the 1st of December.</p>
<p>The coalition is composed of 3 consortia, each dedicated to one aspect of the stack, and all complementary one to another :</p>
<ul>
<li>SuperFOSS: OSes for mobile and desktop, firmware, platform services and protocols, OEM / hardware.</li>
<li>SuperAPPS: decentralized apps like Element, Conversations, app stores, identity, ELFA/NextGraph, Mastodon, Discourse, ERP, Professional social apps, virtual worlds</li>
<li>SuperSAFE: Rust, security, Forges, networks (mesh, VPN, PQC, multipath, edge, secure update), CRA &amp; compliance, web engine and dev frameworks and infratructure</li>
</ul>
<p>A sub-granting program will run throughout the 3 years with a budget of 3.5 M€ to be claimed by contributors from the community, who wish to enrich the existing solutions.</p>
<p>During this presentation we will dive into the vision, the goals, the challenges facing us, and give an overview of the expected outcomes of this comprehensive effort towards a sovereign stack made in Europe, based on open source, and ready for scale.</p>
<p>I will be joined by the co-leads Rik Viergever of E Foundation  and Daniel Thompson-Yvetot of CrabNebula/Tauri who are the coordinators of the consortium.</p>

### Beyond vibe coding: how non-developers build business apps that last

<p>Why AI-built business apps fail, and how non-developers can build ones that last On Friday afternoon, Sophie described an app to an AI. Three weeks later, forty colleagues relied on it. On Tuesday morning, IT shut it down. Nobody got hacked and nobody did anything wrong. The same story could play out in any company where business teams can now build their own tools. Through Sophie's story, told twice, Adrien Maret shows why generating code has become the easy part, and what non-developers and IT teams need to build apps that remain operational a year later, without slowing anyone down or putting the company at risk.</p>

### Amplifying APIs to Visuals for Cost Effective Business Analytics and Financial data

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Design for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Syed Khalid, Syed Usman Ahmad

<p>In the world of information, financial data plays a major role in decision-making for investors as well as companies. This data spans a wide array of sectors and financial instruments, including stock data, income statements, P&amp;L statements, and so on.</p>
<p>Although APIs are the backbone for providing the data foundation, the real challenge comes when they are not in a standardized format that adheres to your specific needs and requirements for your project. In the case of financial applications, this becomes even more critical/complex as the data spans a wide array of types; from numbers and figures to news and images.</p>
<p>This raises the question of how we can effectively consolidate this into a single visual platform capable of providing a holistic solution to consumers while keeping it vendor-agnostic.</p>
<p>In this talk, we will present a single unified market analytics dashboard using APIs with Grafana that can be leveraged by consumers and investors for data-driven decision making. This will be based on open-source tools that will provide an alternative to the costly solutions present in the industry while also avoiding vendor lock-in.</p>
<p>After this session, the audience will have a better understanding of the possibilities and advantages that open-source tools provide in the financial sector and how they can be used with their APIs to create a scalable and robust financial research platform.</p>

### Building public AI on open source: a shared policy agenda for European AI builders

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: OpenLLM Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alek Tarkowski

<p>AI systems built and governed in the public interest, including a state-of-the-art open model and the data commons it depends on. Open source AI developers in Europe have already produced much of the foundation, but their models are underused, underfunded and largely missing from EU policy. This talk places public AI within the broader challenge of sustaining Europe's information ecosystem. AI systems are trained on knowledge produced by publishers, cultural institutions, open communities and the public, while the economic value flows elsewhere and the sources that feed these systems are weakened. Public AI can reverse this dynamic, but only if three challenges are addressed: building and maintaining shared data and model infrastructure, deciding who pays for the commons, for instance through an AI levy, and creating demand from public institutions and other users. The talk will end with a blueprint for a shared policy position that European open source AI developers could take into the debates of the coming years.</p>

### Event Contracts Are Domain Decisions

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Dr. Annegret Junker

<p>Ask an LLM to draft an AsyncAPI document from a domain model and you get a valid file in seconds. In one of our projects, one such draft came back bound to Kafka. The team had decided on RabbitMQ months earlier. Nobody had told the model, and nothing in the domain model could have: a good domain model knows nothing about brokers.
That small mistake points at a bigger question. An AsyncAPI document records decisions: which events exist, what they mean, who may rely on them, and how they evolve. AI can write the YAML. It cannot make those decisions. So where do they come from, and how do they reach the spec intact?
This talk follows the event contracts of Larder, a cooking platform we modeled and built end to end in a project. We start where the events are found: an Event Model that marks which interactions are synchronous and which are asynchronous. We add what a board leaves open with an asynchronous API Product Canvas, a refined Visual Glossary that carries invariants, and an ADR that fixes the broker.
Then AI drafts the AsyncAPI. Every operation description reads like a sentence from the Domain Story, so domain experts can review the contract without translation.
We compare two generated event contracts side by side: one derived from the canvas, one derived straight from the aggregates. We carry SOLID over to event contracts — backward compatibility as Liskov substitution over time, additive evolution with tolerant readers, one AsyncAPI per Bounded Context. And we show what tooling catches and what it cannot: a diff finds a removed field, but only a
check against the Ubiquitous Language finds a field that quietly changed its meaning.
Finally, the contract becomes the spine of implementation: publishers and consumers come from established AsyncAPI code generators and are wired test-first with an outbox relay, and contract tests guard every release.
The message is simple: the promise of a Bounded Context is made by people — in the canvas, the glossary and the decisions. AI writes the boring part: the AsyncAPI document that records it.</p>

### From API Access to Authorized Action: Governing MCP Agents

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Agent Security
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Shamlah Veerapandian

<p>Giving an engineering agent access to an API is easy. Deciding when it should be allowed to act is harder. MCP makes tools available to a model, but teams still need to define who can use them, for which tasks, and with what evidence.</p>
<p>In this talk, I will explore that boundary using lessons from developing a software-factory prototype for enterprise engineering. We will follow a proposed workflow across tickets, repositories, tests and delivery systems, examining scoped permissions, evidence tied to a code revision, independent verification and human approval before consequential actions. A successful tool call does not tell us whether the resulting change should be merged or deployed.</p>
<p>I will explain what local qualification and draft-PR CI have demonstrated, while distinguishing those results from proposed security controls and delivery integrations that remain unresolved. This is a practical discussion of a system still being developed, including its limits and open questions.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with a method for mapping agent-to-API trust boundaries, reviewing the evidence behind an action, and deciding where authorization should be enforced outside the model.</p>

### Observability’s Sixth Sense: Detecting Anomalies in Metrics

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Amphitheater
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Diana Todea

<p>Modern systems produce more metrics than any single person can reason about. As systems grow and change, defining fixed thresholds becomes harder and unexpected behavior often appears without clearly crossing an alert boundary.
In this talk, we look at anomaly detection as a complementary way of working with metrics. Instead of relying on predefined limits, anomaly detection focuses on identifying behavior that deviates from what is normally observed over time.
Using a short, live walkthrough with real metric data, the talk shows how anomalies can surface gradual changes, unusual patterns and subtle shifts that are easy to miss in dashboards. The focus is on how developers can interpret these signals, where anomaly detection is useful and where it is not.
The session is exploratory and practical, aimed at developers who work with metrics and want additional ways to understand system behavior without introducing complex models or heavy tooling.</p>

### Swap the model, keep the agent: open source AI for public services

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jérémie Doucy

<p>Public services cannot bet their AI on a single vendor. Prices change, models disappear with a few months' notice, and the rules on where data may live keep moving. Yet most AI projects are built around one provider, and changing it later means starting over.</p>
<p>At Bayes Impact we took the opposite approach. We build an open source agent platform for public services, and we treat the model as a replaceable part. The same agents run on US commercial APIs and on open models (Mistral, Gemma, Qwen) that we serve with vLLM on our own GPUs in Europe. Changing the model is a configuration change, and we test the agents against each model before every switch.</p>
<p>This talk shares what we learned:</p>
<ul>
<li>which layers of an AI service you must own to stay free to change models</li>
<li>why you may not need the latest frontier model for an assistant that answers questions from your own documents</li>
<li>what really changes when you switch, and how to measure it before your users see it</li>
<li>the real cost of self-hosting open models on GPUs, compared with paying per token</li>
<li>how to explain these choices to decision makers, lawyers and data protection officers</li>
</ul>
<p>The goal is a practical path to digital sovereignty for public organisations: open code, a free choice of model, and no lock-in.</p>

### The AI Agent Era: From Coding to Orchestration

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: AI Gateways and Orchestration
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Akhara Sang

<p>Until recently, working with AI followed a simple pattern: a human asks, the assistant answers. We prompt, validate, and prompt again. It's useful, but every step still depends on our attention.
Something is starting to change. A new pattern is taking shape: human-led, agent-operated. The human sets the intent and the guardrails, then reviews the result. In between, the agent plans, acts, and iterates on its own.
This talk looks at what that shift might mean in practice. How do we write instructions when the AI is going to act, not just answer? How do we build trust in an outcome we didn't watch unfold step by step? And where does this approach actually help, versus where it still falls short?</p>

### The Human Side of Sovereignty: From Shadow AI to Cognitive Defense

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 15:30–15:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Isabelle Galy

<p>What if the real threat to your sovereignty wasn’t technology… but how your people use it?</p>
<p>A new form of conflict is emerging : a cognitive war.
Not fought with weapons, but through the tools we adopt every day.</p>
<p>Employees are not resisting AI. They are embracing it, often through external, uncontrolled tools.  What we call Shadow AI is not deviance. It’s adaptation.</p>
<p>But behind this efficiency lies a shift: decisions influenced, knowledge shaped, dependencies created.</p>
<p>This is not just an IT risk. It’s a strategic vulnerability.</p>
<p>Because sovereignty today is not only about where your data is stored but who is shaping the way your people think.</p>
<p>The real challenge is not to block these tools, but to transform your organization:</p>
<p>From unaware users of Shadow AI to an army of vigilant defenders of your cognitive sovereignty.</p>
<p>Because in a cognitive war, every employee is on the front line.</p>

Rather than a purely academic curiosity, Apertus is intended as commons:  a foundational model that is free, well-understood, well-documented, free of copyright and privacy risks, and can be easily built upon by anyone across the world. ### Never Forward, Always Rebuild: Zero-Trust Payloads for APIs

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 16:30–16:55
- Room: Aubépine 6/7
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Security for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Marcel Grauwen

<p>No Pass-Through: Rebuilding Every API Message at the Trust Boundary
API gateways route traffic and WAFs block known-bad patterns, but what happens when an approved payload carries something nobody anticipated? Cross-domain guards solve this with a stricter rule: never forward a message as received. Parse it, inspect it, and rebuild it clean.
This talk shows how UTL-X, an open-source, format-agnostic transformation language, becomes such a guard. Every incoming message, whether JSON, XML, CSV, YAML or OData, is parsed into one Universal Data Model, and its schema can be checked against an approved contract (XSD, JSON Schema and more). UTL-X 1.1's pure, deterministic validation layer then inspects that tree: field allow-lists, value constraints and cross-field checks. What passes is re-serialized into a strict canonical form, in the same format or a different one. Comments, duplicate keys, smuggled bytes and unexpected fields simply do not survive. What fails is rejected with an audit record.
We'll walk through a concrete example: a partner's JSON order enters, only approved fields are kept, and canonical XML leaves for the internal system. You'll see why this &quot;&quot;parse, inspect, rebuild&quot;&quot; pattern matches NIST and NCSC cross-domain guidance, and how to apply it to your own APIs.</p>

### Stop Throwing Away Your Data - Towards Data-Driven API Governance

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 16:30–16:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Matthias Keckl

<p>A developer bypasses a linting rule to unblock a release, unknowingly shipping a breaking change. Meanwhile, a critical endpoint slows down release after release, never tripping a test timeout, until latency spikes in production. These problems reach production not because teams lack linting or testing tools, but because traditional pre-production checks operate as isolated pass-or-fail gates.</p>
<p>The moment a check finishes, its rich diagnostic data vanishes into terminal outputs or CI logs. While developers focus on passing the current build, engineering leaders remain blind to accumulating technical debt until production monitoring alerts fire. But what if we stopped treating pre-production checks as throwaway gatekeepers and started treating them as continuous quality signals?</p>
<p>In this session, we will explore what specific diagnostic data to collect across each stage of the API lifecycle. We will look at practical patterns for gathering this data without adding friction to daily developer workflows. Finally, we will outline how teams can use this raw execution data to build clear API health and governance signals, enabling leaders to spot trends and offer guidance long before code reaches production.</p>

### Your AI Agent Is Only as Good as Your API Description — Ensuring OpenAPI Quality at Enterprise Scale

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 16:30–16:55
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Design for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Pavel Kornev

<p>AI agents don't read documentation — they read metadata. As API-driven automation and agentic architectures become the norm, the quality of your OpenAPI descriptions is no longer a nice-to-have; it's the foundation that determines whether machines can reliably discover, understand, and orchestrate your services. Yet across the industry, most API descriptions remain incomplete, inconsistent, or outright misleading — and existing linting tools only scratch the surface.</p>
<p>In this talk, we share how a large enterprise manages thousands of APIs while treating quality as a moving target. We'll have a look at the current validation tooling, expose its blind spots, and present the approach we built to enforce rich, machine-consumable API descriptions at scale — without slowing down the hundreds of teams that ship them. You'll learn how decentralized governance, automated validation pipelines, and a shift-left mindset can coexist with high developer velocity, and why getting this right is the single most impactful thing you can do to make your APIs AI-ready.</p>

### Agents Inherit Your Governance — Whether You Meant Them To or Not

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: AI Gateways and Orchestration
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kin Lane

<p>Every API provider in the APIs.io catalog, roughly 25,000 companies, carries two scores derived from years of API Evangelist profiling. The &quot;Kin Score&quot; is a 0–100 composite of governance fundamentals: contracts, documentation, security, support, operations, and a conditional regulatory layer that applies only to banking, health, payments, insurance, securities, and government. Agent Readiness is a separate 0–100 rating across twelve dimensions, measuring whether an autonomous agent can drive the API safely without a human filling in the gaps.</p>
<p>The two are scored independently and never blended. That was a deliberate design choice, and it turned out to be the most useful one, because it lets the catalog answer a question the industry keeps asserting without evidence: does governance actually determine AI readiness, or is agent readiness something you can ship separately?</p>
<p>The data shows that you inherit it, Agent readiness is a layer, and a layer cannot rise above its foundation. The dimensions that separate the agent-native band from everyone else, idempotency, error semantics an agent can branch on, auth clarity, consent and identity, rate-limit signalling, are not AI features. They are governance disciplines that were always supposed to be there, now load-bearing in a way they weren't when a human developer was around to work around them. Meanwhile a very large cluster of providers sits at an identical baseline: a spec, an auth scheme, a rate-limit header. That is the floor of the enrichment pipeline, not readiness.</p>
<p>My talk will walk the composite rubric facet by facet, shows what each one becomes once an agent is the consumer, and then reads the scored catalog for what actually moves the agentic readiness layer. It includes the negative finding: shipping an MCP server on top of an ungoverned API does not move the number, and in a governed portfolio it makes things worse by giving agents a confident interface to an unreliable contract.</p>
<p>As API governance moves from an offensive strategy to a defensive one in a market being flooded with agentic activity, the API foundation you are building on will matter when it comes to adapting to the unpredictable and chaotic market we are all trying to make sense of today.</p>

### Agents Need APIs: Multi-Agent Pipelines in Production

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Emerging API Best Practices
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Camille Epitalon

<p>We run Verso, an agentic platform for qualitative consumer research: voice AI interviews at scale, multi-agent pipelines, and a company brain that agents and humans share. Clients hit us through a UI, a CLI, and MCP — so our APIs are designed for AI callers first, not only humans.</p>
<p>This talk is a production postmortem. I’ll show how we harness multi-agent systems that run thousands of hours of interviews a day: tool contracts, sandboxing, evals, and the failure modes that only show up at volume. I’ll cover what changes when the primary consumer of your API is an agent (idempotency, schemas, observability, rate limits), how MCP and CLI become the real product surface, and how a versioned company brain keeps tacit knowledge in the loop instead of trapped in Slack.</p>
<p>You’ll leave with a concrete checklist for shipping agentic systems that talk to real APIs — less demo magic, more boring reliability. Based on building Verso (seed-backed, enterprise clients) as both an agentic product and a semi-autonomous company run with agents.</p>

### Anthropomorphism is an API: designing the human interface of AI agents

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Design for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Stanislas Bernard

<p>Every API comes with a contract: what it does, what it won't do, and how it fails. AI agents now have a second interface that nobody specifies: the human one. Their name, their tone, how they sign an email, when they take initiative, and whether they admit they are not human.</p>
<p>For the past months, we have run AI chiefs of staff for executives at Theodo. They read mail, prepare meetings, draft replies, and post on Slack. Our users gave them names and faces, and delegation went up. Then the people on the other side of the email started noticing, and some felt fooled.</p>
<p>Why does it matter? Because agents are leaving the chat window. They now act in the channels where humans work, in front of people who never chose to talk to a machine. In that world, anthropomorphism is not a UX gimmick. It is an interface, and an underspecified one breaks trust the way an undocumented breaking change breaks clients. The EU AI Act already makes part of that contract mandatory.</p>
<p>In this talk, I treat the human side of an agent as an API: what to specify, what to version, and what to never fake. You will leave with a concrete checklist to design agent personas that people delegate to without feeling deceived.</p>

### AsyncAPI in the Agentic Era: Giving AI Agents Ears

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Laurent Broudoux

<p>AI agents can call your APIs, but they are blind to what happens in your system. Meanwhile, your Kafka topics, message queues, and AsyncAPI contracts already provide a precise description of everything that happens and everything that can be triggered. So, how do you connect the two?</p>
<p>This session explores the role of AsyncAPI within agentic workflows. We will begin by addressing a specific limitation: the request/response model of the Model Context Protocol (MCP) forces an agent to rely on polling, whereas event-driven architectures are asynchronous by nature. We will examine ongoing developments within the MCP specification—such as subscriptions and notifications—and explore how an AsyncAPI contract can support them. A live demo with the open source reShapr project and Kafka shows it working. We’ll also cover the open questions: delivery semantics, filtering, auditing, tracing and access control.</p>
<p>You’ll leave with a practical lens on which events to expose to agents, and what your AsyncAPI contract needs to become an agent-ready on.</p>

Et que reste-t-il au créateur quand tout le reste est automatisé ?</p>

### Green Architecture: less fat, more impact, more efficiency!

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Thierno Diallo

<p>Have your APIs become a bit bloated? Are they consuming more resources than necessary? Don't panic, we're bringing out the toolbox to give them a green makeover!</p>
<p>In this session, we'll automatically apply the &quot;API Green Score&quot; framework to a real, existing API, using open-source tools, concrete metrics, and a report that everyone can use.</p>
<p>The observation is simple: modern APIs are often resource-intensive.</p>
<p>The problem? Not a lack of principles, but the absence of measurable and automated rules.</p>
<p>In this live session, we will:</p>
<ul>
<li>take measurements (payload, latency, cache),</li>
<li>calculate the Green Score per endpoint,</li>
<li>implement actionable rules: GZIP/Brotli compression, partial fields, streamlining of transferred data, mandatory pagination, etc.,</li>
<li>automate regression testing (SonarQube, Spectral).</li>
<li>A dashboard to make results accessible</li>
</ul>
<p>The result? A lighter, faster API and a reduced environmental impact.</p>
<p>We'll show you the gains live (before/after): reduced network traffic, stabilized latency, and optimized caching.</p>
<p>And to take it with you, a GitHub repository awaits you with everything you need to replicate the process.</p>

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25

- Conference: Conférence des Technoréalistes

### Smaller, Safer, Faster: how we are unlocking the potential of local AI

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: OpenLLM Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: George Drayson

<p>Running capable language models on local hardware is a practical route to sovereign AI, but it raises new research problems that must be tackled. In this talk I will share what we have learned at Locai Labs building models, software and hardware for local inference, structured around three pillars. Compression: making models small enough to run locally through pruning and quantisation, without losing the capabilities that matter. Safety and alignment: making sure compressed models stay safe and aligned once they leave centrally managed infrastructure. Speed: serving many concurrent users and agents on a single machine. For each, I will share our results and the trade-offs we have found between them. I will close with open questions for the future of local AI, including distributed inference and continual learning on-device.</p>

- Date: 2 December 2026

### The Anatomy of an Agentic AI Sandbox

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: What's coming next
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Ludovic Pourrat

<p>Agents are only as useful as the systems they can reach. In a regulated bank, those systems are the hardest ones to open up. So most agentic ideas stall before anyone can find out whether they work.</p>
<p>At Lombard Odier, we took a different approach. We built a sandbox that gives agents everything they need and nothing they can break: realistic APIs, synthetic data, discoverable tools and documentation written for machines as well as people. Teams go from an idea to a working, validated agentic use case in days, without writing code.</p>
<p>When failing is cheap and safe, teams try more ideas, faster.</p>
<p>This talk walks through how the sandbox is built, and where we go next.</p>

### The Easiest Path Wins: Earning Platform Adoption with DDD and Product Thinking

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:25
- Room: Amphitheater
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Fabrizio Lazzaretti, Patrick Müller

<p>Internal platforms rarely fail on technology. They fail because nobody wanted them: golden paths no one walks, tools made mandatory instead of good, dashboards counting onboarded seats, not real usage. A mandated platform gets routed around. A good one gets adopted because it's the easiest path.
The shift that changes everything: your developers are customers you must win, not captives you can assign. Drawing on years building and leading platforms, internal and external, we share an intrapreneur's playbook. Validate demand before you build. Use Domain-Driven Design to align on the real workflow, not the org chart. Use Design Thinking and product management to right-size what you ship, from PoC to MVP to product. Then make it stick with Kubernetes foundations, API-first design, and WebAssembly extensibility so users build on top of it.
You'll walk away with concrete frameworks and methodologies to build the platform people want, and the warning signs that you're going the wrong direction.</p>

### IA agentique : L’Open Source peut-il changer la donne en Europe ?

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:00–17:40
- Room: Acacia 5

### Et si votre IA apprenait la sobriété ?

### Green IT, Smart Business: How City of Copenhagen Cuts Carbon and Costs at the Same Time

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:20–17:45
- Room: Dragonnier 6
- Conference: GreenIO

- Speakers: Kerstin Roberts

<p>At the City of Copenhagen, Green IT is not a cost – it’s a shortcut to efficiency. By rethinking how we buy, run and retire technology, we cut energy use, extend the life of our equipment and reduce waste, while lowering our total IT spend. We use data on both CO₂ and costs to steer decisions, so sustainable choices become the smartest business choices. In Copenhagen, Green IT is a strategic tool: it powers better digital services, protects the climate and strengthens the bottom line at the same time.</p>

À partir de cas concrets, elle introduit le concept d’« IA robuste » et propose des principes actionnables pour concilier performance, innovation et durabilité.</p>

### 60,000 CVEs and Counting: What 60 Years of AppSec Tell Us

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Aubépine 6/7
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Security for AI
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Pedram Hayati

<p>2026 passed 60,000 published CVEs before the end of September, more than any previous full year and roughly 50% up on 2025. But raw vulnerability volume is only part of the story.</p>
<p>Application Security has evolved far beyond just fixing injection flaws. Over the past two decades, it has matured into a complex ecosystem encompassing automated code scanning, secure CI/CD pipelines, cloud-native security posture management, and most recently, AI copilots and autonomous remediation.</p>
<p>In this session, we will walk through the key milestones that have shaped modern AppSec. We will unpack emerging threats, the rise of AISecOps, the growing mandate for SBOMs, and the dual-edged role of AI in both attacking and defending software.</p>
<p>Why does this matter? Vulnerability volume is growing faster than ever, and AI is now both writing and fixing code. Understanding how AppSec got here is essential to deciding what comes next.</p>
<p>You will leave with a clear understanding of today's AppSec landscape, the challenges that lie ahead, and the strategies needed to secure applications in 2026 and beyond.</p>

### AI - Spec-Driven Development: Lessons from £Billions in Payments

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: APIs Governance, Ecosystems and Platforms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Cihan Dogan

<p>Letting AI write code is one thing. Letting it write code that moves real money is another.</p>
<p>This is not a tool demo. It is a year of spec-driven development on a live payment platform that processes billions of pounds in deposits and withdrawals, across seven real projects that are all running in production today.</p>
<p>AI can write a week of code in an afternoon, but most teams still ship at the same speed they always did. Spec-driven development is what closed that gap for us: work that used to take months now takes weeks, on a PCI-regulated payment platform serving 39+ countries.</p>
<p>In our payment team we changed the order of work. We agree the spec first, then let AI write the code. In this talk I walk through two real integrations shipped this way, a secondary provider for Apple Pay and Google Pay built with GitHub Spec Kit, and a new payment method integration delivered in five weeks before the World Cup deadline, with the production numbers behind both.</p>
<p>I will also cover what it takes to make this work at scale: keeping AI focused in a large codebase, where the process slows you down instead of speeding you up, and the honest pros and cons of running this on a system where a mistake costs real money.</p>

### An Open LLM Is Not Enough: Building a Sovereign European AI Stack

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: OpenLLM Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Andrzej Neugebauer

### Best Practices Building Agentic AI GTM

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Emerging API Best Practices
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Andrey Lokot

<p>Everyone is racing to ship agentic AI, but almost nobody talks about what it actually takes to sell it, especially into regulated, risk-averse enterprises like banks. At DataWave, I've spent the last two years building and selling agentic AI systems into financial institutions, and most of the standard AI GTM playbook falls apart on contact with real buyers: procurement teams that don't trust automation, compliance officers who need to explain every decision an agent makes, and champions who need to defend the purchase internally long after the demo ends.This talk shares the practical, sometimes counterintuitive lessons from doing this in production: why we deliberately pushed complexity to the front end instead of hiding it behind a slick agent interface, why full-automation pitches kill deals faster than they close them, and how reframing the sale around augmentation rather than autonomy changed our win rate. Attendees will leave with concrete GTM tactics for positioning, pricing, and pitching agentic AI products to enterprise and regulated buyers, grounded in real deals rather than theory.</p>

### Digital Sovereignty from policy ambition to executive imperative

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Dattier 2
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Guillaume Renaud

<p>Digital sovereignty is no longer just a policy issue. It is a core business imperative, shaping how organizations manage risk, safeguard operations, and compete in an increasingly interdependent digital world. It this session we'll share various customer case study to manage their digital sovereignty from dependency management to minium viable company and sovereign ready operating model.</p>

### From API Sprawl to API Clarity

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: AI Gateways and Orchestration
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Meryem Benaich

<p>APIs have become the backbone of modern digital ecosystems. Yet in many organizations, especially in industrial and regulated environments, API growth outpaces governance. What begins as acceleration quickly turns into fragmentation: undocumented endpoints, unclear ownership, inconsistent standards and rising compliance exposure.</p>
<p>This talk explores a pattern observed across multiple mid-market transformation programs: API sprawl is rarely a technical failure. It is a visibility and prioritization failure. When APIs scale faster than control mechanisms, organizations lose traceability, weaken accountability and increase operational risk.</p>
<p>Drawing from field experience in manufacturing and regulated industries, I will share a practical framework to assess API maturity, identify hidden risk layers and reintroduce structural clarity. Instead of treating governance as bureaucracy, we will examine how it becomes a strategic accelerator — enabling trust, interoperability and AI readiness.</p>
<p>APIs are no longer just integration tools. They are control surfaces that shape how organizations scale, collaborate and remain compliant. Designing them with clarity is no longer optional — it is a condition for sustainable growth.</p>

### From Friction to Platform: Rebuilding a Permission System at Scale

- Date: 2 December 2026

- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays

- Speakers: Elizabeth Zaslavsky

<p>From a fragmented permission system (7 permission types, 17 endpoints, up to 3 weeks lead time) to a ReBAC platform built on OpenFGA — a progressive rewrite, no big bang, onboarding around thirty products and teams. When tech strategy serves a product vision and real business stakes.</p>

### How AI Agents work?

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: GenerationAI
- Track: Understanding GenAI & Agents
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Emmanuel Orozco

<p>AI agents can write code, run tests, and automate workflows. But what’s actually happening under the hood? Surprisingly, it starts with a while loop.</p>
<p>Through step-by-step examples, we’ll see how it chooses an action, executes it, reads the result, and decides what to do next.</p>
<p>You’ll leave with a practical mental model of how agents work, and how to move from using an agent as a black box to understanding, and building, one yourself.</p>
