### Many Agents, One Platform: Production Multi-Agent Apps on K8s with CNCF Building Blocks

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Amphitheater
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Daniel Oh

<p>Multi-agent setups are easy to sketch on a whiteboard and painful to run on a cluster. If you've watched agents chase each other in circles or ship traces that stop at the model boundary, you already know why &quot;more agents&quot; isn't the hard part, but the operations are. Daniel Oh focuses on the boring-but-important layers such as isolation and scaling on Kubernetes, handoffs with CloudEvents, tracing/metrics with OpenTelemetry across model calls and tools, plain gRPC/HTTP between services, guardrails with OPA, SPIFFE/SPIRE where identity actually matters, and KEDA when work piles up asynchronously. Then we run a live Kubernetes demo using cloud-native microservices (Java/Quarkus-style) wiring multiple agents together end-to-end, with traces you can actually follow in Grafana or Jaeger. Walk away with a short implementation checklist, a handful of failure modes worth catching early, and a simple mental model for keeping multi-agent systems maintainable without bolting on bespoke glue.</p>

### OpenAPI: The Answer Key for MCP Tool Calling

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Miguel Quintero

<p>Your MCP server passes its unit tests. Then an agent connects, picks the wrong tool, sends a well-formed date for the wrong week, and reports success. Failures like these come from how a model reads your tool names, descriptions, and schemas, and most teams have no way to measure them before release. This talk argues that the answer key already exists: your OpenAPI description. The same document that generates your MCP tools can grade how agents use them. Examples become golden test cases. JSON Schema becomes argument assertions. HTTP semantics and deprecation metadata become safety gates. We'll walk through a pipeline that lints the spec for agent readiness, generates the tools, scores tool selection, arguments, and task completion, and blocks a release in CI when an unsafe call slips through. You'll leave with a mapping from OpenAPI fields to eval signals you can apply to your own specs, a pipeline that fits into existing CI, and a case for holding destructive and permission-boundary calls to a zero-tolerance threshold instead of a blended pass rate.</p>

### The API Multiverse: One Internet, Infinite Agents, New Rules

- Date: 2 December 2026
- Time: 17:30–17:55
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: What's coming next
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Aura Yavary

<p>The internet was designed for humans to navigate and APIs for applications to communicate. But what happens when autonomous AI agents become the primary users of both?</p>
<p>We are entering the API Multiverse: a world where thousands of intelligent agents discover services, negotiate capabilities, orchestrate workflows, and interact across organizational boundaries. Yet today's API architectures were largely designed around predictable requests, predefined integrations, and human-controlled execution—not autonomous systems that reason, make mistakes, adapt, and act.</p>
<p>This talk explores the technical foundations of an agent-native internet and proposes a practical architecture for building it.</p>
<p>We will examine how API contracts must evolve to expose machine-readable capabilities, how agents can coordinate through dynamic discovery and orchestration, and why identity, authorization, observability, and verification become essential when AI systems initiate real-world actions.</p>
<p>Drawing on research in multimodal world models, autonomous decision-making, and failure-aware agents, the session will introduce an actionable framework for reliable agent–API interactions, including state tracking, failure detection, recovery mechanisms, and human oversight.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with concrete architectural patterns, design trade-offs, and a roadmap for adapting existing API ecosystems to autonomous agents.</p>
<p>The central question is no longer how to connect applications. It is how to build an internet where intelligent agents can act independently without sacrificing reliability, security, or human control.</p>

### From law to code: where to put the human in the loop

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:35
- Room: Dragonnier 3
- Conference: Build It Summit Paris 2026
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Thomas Villaren

<p>How do you build trust in an LLM-based system when its users are responsible for getting compliance right? At PayFit, we have been iterating over the last two years on a “from law to code” workflow with compliance experts: identifying relevant legal changes, extracting payroll parameters, and translating legal requirements into rules. Our main lesson has been to start with what matters to the business and the experts doing the work: which mistakes have consequences, what they need to verify, and where their judgment is essential. Those priorities shape what we automate, what tool we build, how humans stay involved, and how we evaluate the results. Through concrete examples, I’ll share how we involved compliance experts at the right points in the workflow: deciding what needs their attention, what they need to review, and where they should steer the system. We’ll explore what worked, where we need to iterate, and how these choices helped build trust in the outputs.</p>

### AI Goes Off Script: eBPF Guardrails for Kubernetes

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Raymond de Jong

<p>AI workloads may be unpredictable at the application layer, but their effects on infrastructure are observable. Every training job, inference service, and agent ultimately interacts with networks, APIs, processes, and files. This session explores how open-source eBPF technologies can turn those signals into practical guardrails for AI workloads on Kubernetes.</p>
<p>Through a live demonstration, we show how platform and security teams can move from reactive troubleshooting to a repeatable workflow of observing, constraining, detecting, and validating workload behavior. The outcomes include more trustworthy connectivity, reduced exposure of inference services, earlier detection of unexpected model or data access, and Kubernetes-aware evidence for investigating failures and security events.</p>
<p>More importantly, these controls provide a common operating model across conventional microservices and emerging AI workloads. Teams can apply familiar cloud-native identity, policy, and observability practices as AI infrastructure expands across clusters and environments.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with a practical architecture based on Cilium, Hubble, and Tetragon, together with an honest understanding of its boundaries.</p>

### Don't Route What You Can't Redact: Sensitivity-Aware LLM Routing

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Acacia 6
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Christopher Nuland

<p>Semantic routing between LLMs often sacrifices data privacy by prioritizing context over sensitivity when balancing local and SaaS endpoints. For instance, a simple query like &quot;What is Chris Nuland's salary?&quot; can leak sensitive context to an external provider.</p>
<p>We introduce a reference architecture that elevates sensitivity as a primary routing metric, establishing a two-dimensional decision matrix with distinct enforcement paths: direct SaaS egress, redact-then-SaaS, and local-only isolation. Our system uses Microsoft Presidio with custom PII recognizers to pseudonymize sensitive entities (e.g., &quot;Chris Nuland&quot; becomes PERSON_1) before egress. Integrating NeMo Guardrails with vLLM-hosted small language models to form a secure egress point. Preventing unauthorized data leakage when managing shared context between models.</p>
<p>This session provides a demo of this stack, showing how platform engineers can enforce semantic routing with robust data isolation and redaction controls.</p>

### Integrations should be regenerated, not maintained

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Predrag Radenkovic

<p>Coding agents have changed the economics of software development. Developers should expect their organizations to own an order of magnitude more code than today. But unless agents can also maintain software reliably, with humans only supervising the process, the gains from AI will be limited.</p>
<p>Building on Chad Fowler’s idea of Regenerative Software, this talk explores a novel approach: if a coding agent can repeatedly regenerate software from durable information capturing what must not change, that shows the agent understands what it is building. That understanding enables the agent to maintain the software reliably as it grows and evolves, instead of having the agent’s performance degrade as misunderstandings accumulate in the code through incremental maintenance.</p>
<p>We'll explore the topic on practical example of API integration development and maintenance, and how this approach already works in practice.</p>

### So... which stack?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Florian Margaine

<p>Choosing a technology stack for interactive AI apps, in the age of coding agents.</p>
<p>You're starting a greenfield project in 2026: an interactive AI application with live UI updates, background workers, and agents talking to LLM providers. Your team is polyglot, and a coding agent writes most of the code — so which technology stack do you actually pick?</p>
<p>This talk tours the major ecosystems — Symfony, Laravel, Phoenix, Django, .NET, Go, Node.js, and Java — through three maturity lenses: AI/agentic support, background workers, and interactive UI. Spoiler: every ecosystem has a weak leg, and there is no perfect-score stack.</p>
<p>Then we get to the real point: the deciding factor has shifted. Before coding agents, team expertise picked the winner — you went with what you knew. Now that agents write the code and reading fluency is trainable, the question is no longer &quot;how well can we write this?&quot; but &quot;how well can we run this?&quot; — hosting support, laptop-friendliness, and operating cost become the tiebreakers. And since switching stacks is cheaper than ever, your platform choices shouldn't be the thing that locks you in.</p>

### The future of mobile OS: open-source, privacy-safe and sovereign

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Rik Viergever

<p>European organizations are moving cloud and collaboration to open source, but not their phones. Over 80% of employees use a smartphone for work, and nearly all run American operating systems under non-European jurisdiction. US sanctions reach European companies and even the International Criminal Court.</p>
<p>The phone belongs at the start of a sovereign stack. This talk shows how /e/OS, a European AOSP-based OS with 100,000+ active users and pilots across Europe’s public and private sectors, combined with European-hosted or self-hosted MDM, gets you there. You leave with a smartphone sovereignty checklist: what works, what doesn’t yet, and concrete next steps.</p>

### Token My Breathe Away: Distributed LLM Serving with llm-d

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Edoardo Vacchi

<p>Chasing LLM providers is a game of cat and mouse: they often change billing strategies and rate limits, they sunset models and rename tiers. And you have to put trust into handing over your customers' data.</p>
<p>But what is the alternative? Serving LLMs in production is a real engineering challenge: hardware is expensive, resource utilization is unpredictable, latency is highly variable.</p>
<p>Becoming a &quot;token producer&quot; demands a radically different approach to infrastructure.</p>
<p>In this technical session, we explore llm-d: an open-source, Kubernetes-native, distributed inference framework.</p>
<p>llm-d's workload-aware router schedules requests by factoring in KV-cache state, load and latency; moreover, llm-d supports advanced optimization techniques, such as offloading KV cache to the CPU, and disaggregating the prefill and decode inference phases to improve throughput.</p>
<p>Join us to learn how to serve LLMs efficiently and reliably on your very own infrastructure!</p>

### Your Coding Agents Need a Platform, Not a Laptop!

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:40
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Engin Diri

<p>Coding agents are spreading through companies the way cloud infrastructure once did: one personal account at a time, each set up however its owner saw fit. In practice, that means one developer's agent can read every credential on their laptop, while another runs with its permission prompts switched off because they slowed the work down.</p>
<p>The last time this happened, the answer was internal developer platforms: paved roads of shared tooling and guardrails that made the sanctioned way also the fastest way. Coding agents now need their own paved road.</p>
<p>That paved road is a background-agent platform: a shared service where agents take tasks, work asynchronously in isolated environments, and return their changes as pull requests. I will introduce this model through OpenInspect, an open-source implementation, tracing what happens from a request in Slack to the resulting pull request.</p>
<p>You will see what the platform adds along the way, and how it lowers the technical barrier for product owners, customer experience teams, and others to turn their domain knowledge into proposed changes to code, documentation, and configuration.</p>

### Develop Today, Deploy Tomorrow: An Interoperability Spec for AI Models

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:15–09:45
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Lauri Apple, Andrew Block

<p>Packaging solves distribution. It doesn’t solve interoperability.</p>
<p>While OCI artifacts and projects like ModelPack provide a foundation for packaging AI models, moving those models from a developer laptop into production Kubernetes environments still requires stitching together metadata, trust, provenance, and deployment workflows.</p>
<p>In this session, we’ll demonstrate how the CNCF Developer Experience TAG is building a lightweight interoperability profile that complements existing standards rather than replacing them. By walking through each component in detail and illustrating the concepts in a live demo, we’ll package, sign, publish, validate, and deploy an AI model from local development to Kubernetes inference using today’s cloud native tooling.</p>
<p>Audience members will leave with a practical blueprint for cloud native AI delivery and a better understanding of how OCI, GitOps, Sigstore, Model Openness Framework, and emerging interoperability standards fit together.</p>

### Salt, Fat, Sugar, Data

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:30–10:05
- Room: Dragonnier 6
- Conference: GreenIO
- Type: Keynote
- Speakers: Tom Greenwood

<p>For the first time in history, humans now live in a food system designed not for health or sustainability, but for addiction and profit. Fast food is engineered to stimulate a dopamine response through the constant drip of refined salt, sugars, and fats, stimulating our cravings for instant pleasure and contributing to a myriad of chronic health conditions. This pattern is mirrored in our digital world that now erodes our mental and societal wellbeing. This talk will explore how the industrialisation of food is now mirrored in the industrialisation of information, and what we can do about it.</p>

<p>Finance tools used to be apps you log into. With its AI Marketplace and MCP, Qonto is turning its product into a platform its customers can build on, connecting their own AI agents and workflows to their business banking. Sophie Cornay, who leads Qonto AI, shares the vision behind this shift and what it takes to open financial data to what customers build. Arnaud Serfaty, Head of Fund Controlling at Everstone REM, brings the customer side: how he uses Qonto's multi-organisation MCP to run the finances of several entities with AI, what he has built so far, and where AI fits in a finance team's daily work.</p>

### APIs as the Nervous System: Building a Trusted, Resilient Agent Fleet on Your Own Infrastructure

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Tuhin Mitra

<p>&quot;Every engineering team has a Slack channel where requests go to die. Bug reports without repro steps, doc questions that take hours to answer, access requests that bypass the audit trail. We built a fleet of agents that resolves them autonomously — wired entirely through APIs, designed to degrade gracefully, and running on infrastructure we control.</p>
<p>The system classifies every incoming message and routes it to a specialised pipeline. Documentation questions trigger an agentic retrieval loop that calls the GitHub Code Search API, Confluence CQL, and curated FAQ endpoints iteratively — no vector index, no embeddings, just API calls and reasoning. Bug and feature reports pass an actionability gate before dispatching to a code exploration pipeline that calls the JIRA REST API to create a ticket and the GitHub REST API to open a draft PR.</p>
<p>Resilience is built into every API boundary. Each pipeline node fails independently — a failed GitHub PR call doesn't roll back the JIRA ticket or silence the Slack reply. An offload mode dispatches to GitHub Actions via workflow_dispatch, so agent commits are server-signed and pass protected branch policies. PII is stripped before any external API receives user data. Prompt injection is neutralised before it reaches the LLM.</p>
<p>This is a direct answer to Black Box Dependency: the brains of this system call your APIs, run on your infrastructure, and operate under your rules. The talk covers the API design decisions that made it production-ready, the failure modes we didn't anticipate, and what sovereign agentic architecture looks like in practice.</p>

### Agents Are Not Missing Tools. They Are Missing Business Context

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kseniia Ilchenko

<p>For years, we’ve talked about data governance and decisions based on trusted data. Then agentic AI arrived, and we started giving agents access to MCP tools with a one-line description, expecting them to figure out how our business works.
An API spec tells an agent what to call. It doesn’t tell it what “active user” means, who owns that definition, or who is allowed to access the data.
Consider an agent asked to identify inactive customers for a retention campaign. Does “inactive” mean no login in 30 days, no purchase in 90 days, or an expired subscription? Should enterprise accounts be treated differently? Every tool works as expected, and the agent still produces the wrong list and triggers the wrong campaign.
Humans know these things because they understand the business and its unwritten rules. Agents don’t. They guess. And when an agent gets it wrong, it may have already taken action before anyone notices.
Adding an agent isn’t the hard part. Giving it the right business context is. That means making definitions, data lineage, ownership, and access rules explicit, and ensuring they’re enforced by the systems agents use rather than left for the model to infer.
Drawing on our experience building an MCP server that provides governed business context to agents, I’ll walk through how we approached it: choosing which tools an agent should have access to, grounding its decisions in reliable context, and deciding when it has enough information to act - and when it should ask instead of guess.</p>

### Agents on Edge: Agentic access with K8s on DGX Spark

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kishore Karthikeyan

<p>AI agents that manage Kubernetes usually hold a powerful, long-lived kubeconfig. This session shows how to remove it, so that every agent action uses a short-lived identity, is limited by RBAC and is recorded in the audit log under the agent's name, even on an edge system without a reliable network connection.</p>
<p>Most AI deployments of the Kubernetes MCP server give the agent a kubeconfig or a long-lived service account token. That credential outlives the session, and any code the agent runs can read it. This talk replaces it with Keycloak, running locally on a single-node edge cluster on an NVIDIA DGX Spark where the uplink cannot be relied on.</p>
<p>AI agent gets a short-lived JWT-SVID from a local SPIRE server and uses it to authenticate to Keycloak through federated client authentication. Keycloak issues a token whose audience is the MCP server, following the MCP authorization specification and Keycloak's MCP integration guide. The MCP server never forwards that token to the Kubernetes API. It exchanges it with Keycloak for a token that the API server accepts through its OIDC authentication configuration, with the agent's SPIFFE ID as the Kubernetes user name. RBAC limits each agent, the MCP server configuration blocks Secrets and destructive verbs, and the Kubernetes audit log records every call under the agent's identity.</p>
<p>Partway through the demo the uplink is disconnected to show what keeps working with Keycloak and SPIRE on the node. The talk closes with the edge-specific problems met along the way, including key caching while offline and clock skew, and with how the same Keycloak configuration carries over from one node to a fleet of edge clusters.</p>

### Beyond REST: Evolving Arazzo for Real-World Agentic Workflows

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Naresh Jain

<p>A real-world business workflow rarely stays within the boundaries of a single API style.</p>
<p>An order might begin with an HTTP API described using OpenAPI, trigger an event described using AsyncAPI, invoke a Protobuf RPC service, query data through GraphQL, and eventually receive an asynchronous notification. Agentic applications make this even more important: an agent needs to understand not just individual API operations, but how capabilities across heterogeneous systems can be composed into a meaningful workflow.</p>
<p>Arazzo was created to describe sequences of API calls and the dependencies between them. But what happens when the next step isn’t another OpenAPI operation?</p>
<p>In this talk, we’ll look at how Arazzo is evolving to support heterogeneous API interaction models. Drawing on the work to introduce AsyncAPI, Protocol Buffer RPC and GraphQL support into the Arazzo Specification, we’ll explore some of the surprisingly difficult design questions that emerge when REST-style request/response assumptions disappear.</p>
<p>How do you represent send and receive operations in an event-driven system? What does a workflow step mean when an RPC is server-streaming or bidirectional? How should GraphQL queries, mutations and subscriptions participate in a workflow? And how can we add these capabilities without turning Arazzo into a collection of protocol-specific special cases?</p>
<p>We’ll use these questions to explore a broader idea: Arazzo can become a protocol-independent description of how API capabilities are composed to accomplish an outcome.</p>
<p>That has implications beyond API documentation and testing. For AI agents, an Arazzo workflow can provide something increasingly valuable: an explicit, machine-readable description of which capabilities to invoke, in what sequence, how data flows between them, and what successful execution looks like.</p>
<p>Rather than asking agents to infer workflows from a collection of disconnected API descriptions, we can start making the workflow itself part of the contract.</p>

### Building a World Model for a $14 Trillion Industry: Why LLMs Alone Are Not Enough

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Fresh thinking from industry leaders
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Saurabh Mishra

<p>What would it take to build an AI system that actually understands an industry rather than merely generating text about it?</p>
<p>Infrastructure and construction provide an especially demanding test. Projects extend across decades, cross national borders and involve interdependent contractors, financiers, suppliers, locations, regulations and risk events. The relevant knowledge is fragmented, constantly changing and often contradictory.</p>
<p>At Taiyo.AI, we have built the intelligence layer for this physical economy using millions of project records, large-scale procurement intelligence, entity relationships, geospatial signals and specialized AI agents.</p>
<p>This session explores the architecture and lessons behind that effort:</p>
<ul>
<li>Context: How to organize heterogeneous data into domain-specific representations with temporal, spatial and entity relationships.</li>
<li>Agents: How to orchestrate specialized reasoning workflows across opportunity discovery, project analysis, procurement and risk.</li>
<li>Harness: How evidence provenance, citations, uncertainty, tool interfaces and evaluations make agents more reliable.</li>
<li>Loops: How feedback, institutional knowledge and continuous data updates improve the system over time.</li>
</ul>
<p>The underlying thesis is that frontier models are a necessary component of enterprise AI, but they are not a substitute for the structured intelligence infrastructure needed to make consequential decisions.</p>
<p>The discussion will also explore architectural limitations, trade-offs between models and data, and the emerging opportunity for domain-specific world models across the physical economy.</p>

### From Mermaid to SQL: Agents built by Experts, Not Engineers

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Hervé Bourzeix, Daniel McDonald

<p>At our adtech platform, troubleshooting an ad campaign used to mean opening various tools, joining multiple datasets in your head, and hunting down the one colleague who knew why the campaign isn't performing as expected. We wanted to give that &quot;colleague&quot; to every campaign manager, but we quickly hit a wall: the people who know how to troubleshoot are not software engineers. To solve this, we stopped writing hard-coded agents laden with business logic and let our domain experts become the authors, using a decision-tree-diagram-first approach backed by rules and contextual knowledge. The resulting system uses a prompt management layer that lets experts draft, preview, refine, and publish their specialised agents, which our Campaign Manager can use seamlessly directly from our buying interface.</p>
<p>Under the hood, an orchestrator agent walks these Mermaid decision trees and hands off tasks to product-specialized sub-agents, which don't need to know what data exists or in what shape, only what question to ask, through a in-house tool that turns natural language into SQL. This translation engine (NL2SQL) behind the scene is built for production safety: an LLM generates SQL constrained to tenant-scoped Table-Valued Functions (TVFs), having every query checked by an AST parser, validated by a BigQuery dry-run, and finally executed. At each stage, a bounded retry loop feeds errors back into the prompt for self-correction.</p>
<p>In this talk, we'll break down the orchestrator/sub-agent topology and explain why it changed our internal contribution model. We'll also dive deep into the security layer, showing how SQL AST validation and TVF-based governance make NL2SQL safe for multi-tenant environments. Finally, you'll leave with a concrete, reusable pattern for building expert-authored systems where engineers are no longer the bottleneck for domain logic.</p>

### From Pilot to Production: The Enterprise AI Enablement Stack

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Frank Kilcommins

<p>Scaling AI is an enablement problem, not just a model problem. This session introduces five foundational steps to make enterprise APIs AI-ready: (1) surface more tools/APIs systematically, (2) measure and describe them better, (3) enable dynamic discovery, (4) define capabilities as business-aligned workflows, not just endpoints, and (5) provide safe, governed execution environments. It reframes readiness as a lifecycle discipline and shows how to turn pilots into durable, governed capabilities using open standards that protect sovereignty.</p>
<p>Takeaways: The five-step API enablement stack; why API exposure alone doesn't scale; how standards-based workflows and governance make AI repeatable in production; patterns for measuring and improving API AI-readiness at scale.</p>

### Platform Obesity, not Complexity, is killing our platforms

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Joep Piscaer

<p>We like to say Kubernetes platforms fail because they’re “too complex”. But complexity isn’t the problem. Platforms fail because they’re obese.</p>
<p>They’re bloated with an excess of features, tools, abstractions, and opinions that far exceed an organization’s operational capacity and cognitive load—especially in enterprises, regulated environments, and talent-constrained teams.</p>
<p>The cloud-native ecosystem doesn’t help. It hands out sweets constantly: one more controller, one more abstraction, one more “best practice.” Each addition seems harmless in isolation. Saying yes is easy. Saying no is career-limiting: since no-one wants to admit their own reality doesn't match the industry narrative. The Pluralistic Ignorance is real, yo.</p>
<p>The ecosystem rewards addition, not subtraction. Often, “simplification” efforts do the opposite—layering abstractions on top of abstractions until the platform is heavier, slower, and harder to operate and change than before.</p>
<p>Eventually, the platform collides with reality: finite talent, finite attention, finite time. Cognitive load exceeds capacity. Operational friction grows. Engineering quality cracks. Business outcomes stall. ROI quietly evaporates.</p>
<p>This isn’t a tooling failure. It’s a constraint failure.</p>
<p>So how do you fix an obese platform? The same way you fix obesity: by creating a calorie deficit, rigorous exercise and discipline. In the platform world, that means recognizing constraints and designing for and staying within those limits, across technology, processes, organizational culture, budget, engineering skills, team cognitive load and more.</p>
<p>Dare to play the hard 'less is more' subtraction game, not the easy game of addition: treat dealing with constraints, subtraction, prioritization and trade-offs as first-class engineering skills—not as signs of lack of ambition.</p>

### Prototyping tomorrow's API: inside IGN's proof-of-concept factory

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Europe API Conference
- Type: Keynote
- Speakers: Valentine Bruyerre

<p>Public institutions hold authoritative data, yet AI assistants cannot reliably answer questions without access to it. How can a public operator make its APIs usable by AI, and test such ideas quickly before committing to large-scale development?</p>
<p>This talk presents IGNfab, the proof-of-concept and demonstrator factory of the French National Institute of Geographic and Forest Information (IGN). A team of engineers works with ministries, public operators and local authorities, with a light, iterative process: define the need, scope the experiment, build a prototype, then deliver a solid basis for deciding whether to scale up.</p>
<p>We will use one of IGNfab's experiments as a case study. Two open source MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, Geocontext and Carto, connect the Géoplateforme, the infrastructure behind cartes.gouv.fr, to any AI assistant. Users can query reference geodata in natural language, for example to locate every school in a municipality or find homes within 3 km of a point, and generate maps without writing a GIS query.</p>
<p>Why it matters: public data is only valuable if people and machines can use it. Other experiments are under way, such as 3D territory visualization and AI-based flood mapping, showing how rapid prototyping helps API providers prepare for the AI era.</p>

### The Cognitive Firm: Designing Human Control into AI Workflows

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API for AI Governance
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jorge Fábrega

<p>Delegation to AI systems reduces the effort devoted to producing an output, yet it raises the demands placed on problem formulation, task decomposition, evaluation, integration, and accountability. The central organizational question is therefore how cognitive work is redistributed when AI enters a workflow, and whether humans retain the capacity to understand, assess, and justify the resulting output.</p>
<p>In this talk, I develop the idea of The Cognitive Firm as an organizational architecture for allocating cognitive operations between humans and AI. Its central concern is Epistemic Ownership: the extent to which people remain able to direct a task, recognize the basis of its results, and incorporate those results into decisions for which they remain responsible.</p>
<p>Evidence from the Anthropic Economic Index suggests that this redistribution differs across modes of use. API-based use is associated with human direction being located further upstream, where objectives, constraints, and validation rules are embedded in technical design. Conversational systems retain more direction within an iterative exchange, where users refine, evaluate, and accumulate understanding during execution. These patterns suggest that automation does not simply remove human cognitive work. It changes where that work occurs and which capabilities become organizationally decisive.</p>
<p>My talk translates this trade-off into principles for designing AI workflows that increase productive delegation without hollowing out human judgment.</p>

### The Reliability Problem in AI Systems

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Les Jackson

<p>Modern AI systems involve orchestrating multiple LLM calls, tool calling, human approvals, and long-running tasks. The challenge now isn’t getting an LLM to produce a coherent answer, it’s everything that happens around it.</p>
<p>Failure is inevitable, especially in cloud native environments where services timeout, containers restart, pods are rescheduled, and external dependencies become unavailable. So how do we build AI systems that continue making progress when failure is simply part of normal operation?</p>
<p>In this session we'll explore why reliability has become one of the defining challenges of production AI systems, and why traditional request-response programming struggles once work spans minutes, hours, or even days.</p>
<p>Using practical examples, we'll examine architectural patterns for building AI systems that can survive failure, recover automatically, coordinate long-running work, and continue making progress without sacrificing the elasticity and resilience expected of cloud native applications.</p>
<p>The focus isn't on prompt engineering or choosing the latest model. It's on designing AI systems that behave predictably in production, where failures are inevitable and reliability matters just as much as intelligence.</p>

### The exit test: mapping dependencies and rebuilding freedom of choice

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 09:45–10:10
- Room: Dragonnier 4
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Veronika Mazour Mestrallet

<p>Digital sovereignty becomes very concrete when organizations try to reduce their dependency on Microsoft.
For many, that dependency is not limited to Microsoft 365 itself. It may span Exchange or Outlook, Teams, SharePoint, identity, document management, integrations, workflows, infrastructure and, increasingly, AI. Some organizations are fully in Microsoft 365; others still operate a mix of cloud, legacy and on-premise components.
Before choosing an alternative, the first challenge is to understand where the dependencies actually are. Then comes the exit test: how can we change today, and will the architecture we choose leave us free to change again tomorrow?
Drawing on real-world Microsoft 365 migration and replacement projects, this talk addresses both questions: first by mapping the dependencies that make exit difficult, then by assessing the target architecture across five key dimensions:
·	Infrastructure and deployment: can the workplace move between SaaS, private cloud and on-premise environments, and how much control does the organization retain over the infrastructure it depends on?
·	Data and knowledge: can information be accessed, migrated and reused independently of the platform that currently hosts it?
·	APIs, open standards and integration with the information system: how open are the interfaces connecting the workplace to identity, email, office tools, chat, business applications and other systems?
·	AI models and services: can organizations change model, provider or inference infrastructure while retaining control over their data, knowledge base and governance framework?
·	User experience and changeability: can users realistically move to another environment without unacceptable disruption, loss of productivity or excessive change-management cost?
Openness does not mean that every component can be swapped instantly or that a digital workplace should become a patchwork of independent tools. A coherent user experience requires integration, and integration inevitably creates dependencies. Even when solutions use the same open standards, replacing one component may require adaptation, testing and engineering.
But there is a fundamental difference between dependencies that are understood, manageable and reversible and lock-in that removes the ability to change.
The challenge is to combine both: an integrated, all-in-one experience that removes complexity for users and buyers, and an underlying architecture open enough to preserve meaningful choices over time.
AI makes this question even more urgent. As models and agents become embedded into everyday work, organizations risk adding a new layer of dependency around proprietary APIs, models and infrastructure before they have solved the previous ones.
Digital sovereignty is not about choosing the “right” provider today. It is about preserving the freedom to choose another one tomorrow.</p>

### Curl a GPU Into Your Cluster: Hybrid Kubernetes for AI Development

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Piotr Zaniewski

<p>AI development has a cluster problem: laptop Kubernetes like kind can't host real GPUs, so engineers juggle Docker Compose, full clusters, and scripts that drift from production. We show a pattern where one laptop kubectl context spans local Docker workers and a cloud NVIDIA T4, joined by a single curl over a WireGuard tunnel, so the same workload runs locally and on cloud GPU without two separate setups.</p>
<p>The idea is Kubernetes-native: any Linux host (KVM, GCE, bare metal) becomes a node by pasting a one-line curl that installs kubelet and connects over the tunnel, in one tenant cluster. We demo it live: laptop Docker workers, a local KVM node, and a GCE T4 in a single context. We pull llama3.2 onto the T4, deploy a service that talks to it, expose it through a built-in LoadBalancer, and pause/resume the cluster like a laptop.</p>
<p>We close with the failure modes (tunnel reconnect after sleep, egress cost on model pulls, GPU eviction) and the GitOps that keeps the topology declarative.</p>

### Manufacturing European Software: From Regulation to Working Practice

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Daniel Thompson-Yvetot

<p>The Cyber Resilience Act changes what it means to ship software in Europe. Manufacturers now carry obligations that reach from design and development through vulnerability handling and end of support. This talk walks through that shift using three connected books written from inside the standardisation process.
Manufacturing European Software sets the foundation. It maps the CRA's essential requirements, the harmonised standards emerging under mandate M/606, and what conformity actually demands of development teams. The talk draws primarily on this book, tracing how a software project becomes a regulated product and what that means for engineering culture, documentation, and supply chains.
Modular Compliance then examines the various means of demonstrating conformity, covering Modules A, B and C, and H, alongside EUCC certification and assessment by a conformity assessment body. Risk, Evidence and Conformity addresses the Default category specifically, providing a complete methodology for building the Binder that carries a product through self-assessment to a credible declaration of conformity.
Attendees leave with a practical picture of the road from legal text to daily practice. Copies of Manufacturing European Software will be given away at a book signing directly after the talk.</p>

### No tool has ever fixed a testing problem

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Aubépine 7

### Risk is Governed. Data Resilience is Architected.

- Date: 3 December 2026

- Type: Talk

<p>A reflection on the alignment — or misalignment — between board-level risk governance and the architectural reality of data systems.
Risk is discussed in boardrooms.
Resilience is implemented in systems.
But too often, the two operate in parallel universes.
What does it mean to treat resilience not as a technical feature, but as a strategic discipline?
How do we translate regulatory accountability into architectural patterns?</p>
<p>Where does operational resilience truly live — in policy documents, or in recovery-tested systems?</p>
<p>And how do we bridge the gap between governance language and data-layer implementation?</p>
<p>This session explores how to structure resilience as a strategic capability — one that is designed, measured, tested, and owned.</p>

### Taking the risk out of AI-written software, without slowing down

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Vincent Verdet

<p>Most industries outside Big Tech are wary of AI-written code, and with reason: more developers distrust its output than trust it. Yet the speed is real. The question for IT leaders is how to take the speed without the risk.</p>
<p>I answered it by building Turbo EA, an open-source Enterprise Architecture platform, as one architect working with AI coding agents: about 400,000 lines of code of enterprise-grade software in eight months, covered by more than 12000 automated tests. AI is probabilistic, a turbulent flow. Software has to be laminar. This talk shows the five rules that straighten the flow without slowing it down: a stack the AI knows, written guidelines, reuse principle, security layers and automation. It also shows the CI/CD pipeline that enforces them, itself generated by AI.</p>
<p>You will leave with a risk framework you can explain to your organization.</p>

### Tokens are not compute: Getting more from LLMs by using them less

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Fresh thinking from industry leaders
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jens Neuse

<p>Most agents today use APIs the same way: every endpoint becomes a tool, every tool definition sits in the context window, and every call goes through the model, one at a time, with each result read back in. We've turned a language model into a CPU and tokens into our unit of compute. It's slow, expensive, and the agent picks the wrong operation more often than anyone admits. The problem isn't MCP, it's how we're looping over tool calls.</p>
<p>Over the last few months, we've tested a different pattern against APIs like GitHub, Linear, Pylon and Circleback: a frontier model writes code against typed SDKs, and we run that code in a sandbox. The model never sees API credentials, and the context window stays clear of intermediate results. If a step needs to classify or summarize, the script calls a smaller model. It's just another API call.</p>
<p>The most interesting part: we can now iterate on the script until the outcome is what we want, like any other program. Running it again costs cents, because the frontier model is out of the loop, and it finishes much faster.</p>
<p>In this talk, we'll walk you through our experiments, including the ones that failed, and what they taught us about how agents actually call APIs.
We're also open-sourcing our solution, so you'll walk away with a concrete example to try, a way to measure cost and efficiency for agentic tasks, design choices that make your REST, GraphQL and MCP APIs not just callable but agent-friendly, and a sense of where the model belongs in the loop and where it doesn't.</p>

### Using NATS.io as a distributed blackboard system

- Date: 3 December 2026

- Room: Acacia 3

<p>Blackboard systems are a design pattern and architectural model, that independent AI agents or experts can use to share state (for example as a digital twin) and coordinate with each other to reach a common goal. This talk will show an example of a peer-to-peer application using NATS.io as a blackboard system and explain why it's uniquely suited (amongst other messaging, queuing and streaming systems) to support this kind of use cases.</p>

### Why SREs and AI Agents Are a Perfect Match – Real demos, real infrastructure, real failures

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 10:15–10:40
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Paul Stack

<p>We all have a list we will never get to but really want to. The infrastructure someone left in a state that'll take a day to untangle. Toil that's always urgent, never prioritised, and deeply unsexy.</p>
<p>AI agents are built for this work. Not because they're smarter than you, but because they don't get bored, they don't skip steps, and they can hold the state of a hundred resources in context while methodically working through each one.</p>
<p>Most teams get this wrong from the start: pointing an agent at infrastructure with raw API access and hoping for the best. That's not engineering. That's gambling. You wouldn't hand a new hire root access on their first day. Don't do it with an agent either.</p>
<p>Add guardrails that encode how your infrastructure works, what operations are safe, what order things need to happen in — and the agent becomes predictable. Deterministic output from a probabilistic system. Those same guardrails solve another problem too. Your most critical systems knowledge lives in the heads of one or two people. When they leave, that knowledge goes with them. Encode it in guardrails that agents can execute and the bus factor drops to zero. Anyone on the team can operate systems they've never touched, because the guardrails know what the human used to.</p>
<p>Real demos, real infrastructure, real failures. This talk shows how SREs and platform engineers can pair with AI agents to tackle the work that's been rotting in the backlog — and why the SRE skill set is exactly what agents need.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang made the point that when AI entered radiology, demand for radiologists went up, not down. The same thing is about to happen to SREs. Agents handle the toil. You focus on the work that actually needs a human. You're not being replaced. You're being empowered.</p>

<p>BO&amp;MIE, the creative bakery brand founded in 2017, runs more than 25 shops in Paris, Aix-en-Provence, Bordeaux, Barcelona, Jeddah, Seoul, Riyadh and Kuwait City. Rather than buying software, CEO Jean-François Bandet chose to build the company's tools in-house with AI: an ERP for stock, purchasing and sales, and a Marketing Studio that runs Google listings, customer reviews, advertising, product visuals and local SEO. Both are built and used every day with AI agents such as Claude Code and Codex, without a dedicated developer team. Jean-François shares what he has learned in the field about how AI concretely changes the way a physical retail business is run and grown.</p>

### A Valid API Call, An Invalid Outcome - Accountability in the Age of AI Agents

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:00–11:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Afsha

<p>AI agents no longer only recommend actions. They act through APIs (application programming interfaces). They can update records, provision infrastructure and trigger business workflows, potentially without a person reviewing each decision. APIs are becoming the hands through which AI agents affect people and organisations.</p>
<p>Technical reliability matters, but it is no longer enough. A valid API call can create an invalid human outcome. Authentication, authorisation and schema validation can show that a request is permitted and correctly formed. They cannot tell us whether the action is wise, fair or ethical.</p>
<p>This talk examines two responsibilities we cannot outsource to AI: ethical judgement and accountability, and human connection and transparency. What happens when a technically valid action produces an unacceptable outcome? When something goes wrong, can the person affected understand what happened, challenge the decision and reach a human who can put it right?</p>
<p>I will introduce my SCOPE framework, a practical checklist for deciding what an agent should be allowed to do before it ever gets access. By the end, attendees will be well placed to shape agentic API systems that are secure and reliable while remaining transparent, accountable and deserving of the autonomy we give them.</p>
<p>This is a beginner-friendly talk designed for both technical and non-technical audiences. No coding or API experience is needed. Whether you build these systems, lead the teams that do, or are simply affected by them, everyone is welcome.</p>

### AI Sovereignty Is an Architecture Problem, Not a Model Choice - Keeping Data, Policy and Execution Under Your Control

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:00–11:25
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: New Paradigms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Giona Granchelli

<p>AI sovereignty is often reduced to one question: where does the model run? But running an open model locally does not automatically give an organisation control over its AI system.</p>
<p>As AI applications become more agentic, models are no longer just generating text. They access data, call APIs, choose tools and trigger actions. This creates a much broader sovereignty problem: who controls what data can leave the system, which models may process it, what actions are allowed, and which policies are enforced before execution?</p>
<p>This talk explores AI sovereignty as an architectural problem rather than a model choice. Drawing from lessons learned while building TramAI, an open-source Kotlin runtime for governed AI workflows, I will show how to separate policy, data boundaries, model routing and tool permissions from the models themselves.</p>
<p>We will look at architectures where sensitive workloads can remain local, cloud models can still be used when policy allows it, dangerous actions can require explicit approval, and every important decision remains observable and auditable.</p>
<p>The goal is not to eliminate cloud AI. It is to build systems where models, providers and infrastructure can change without giving up control over data, policy or execution.</p>

### Commitment Issues: What Git Forgets

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:00–11:25
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Lizzie Siegle

<p>Last month, one of our engineers watched her agent try five different solutions to fix the same bug. Four of them went nowhere, and the fifth finally worked. It got committed, but by the next morning, nobody could reconstruct why the first four methods hadn't worked. That is something that git doesn't keep — it keeps the diff, and throws away the journey (which some say it's all about, instead of the destination.)</p>
<p>I'll walk through what Entire's built to fix that: a unit richer than a commit that holds the agent session, the prompts, and a real account of how much of a change is agent-written versus human-edited, all versioned right alongside the code. We'll see it shown live, pulling up a real checkpoint, a commit trailer, and the line-by-line breakdown of who actually wrote what.</p>
<p>None of this replaces commits, and I'll be upfront about where it shouldn't try to. But if your team is shipping agent-written code and still reviewing it like it's 2019, this is the gap you're going to hit.</p>

### The API Is Not the Authority: Governing Autonomous AI

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:00–11:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Pedro Barbas

<p>Agentic AI is moving from generating recommendations to taking consequential actions: calling APIs, initiating payments, changing infrastructure, placing orders, and triggering enterprise workflows.</p>
<p>That creates a control problem that authentication, API permissions and conventional policy engines do not fully solve. Knowing who an agent is, which API it can call, or what scope it has been granted is not the same as proving that this exact action is authorised, through this route, against this target, under the enterprise’s current state.</p>
<p>This talk explores an execution-control model for autonomous systems built around a simple distinction: access is not authority.</p>
<p>Before execution, the system must bind current authority to the precise protected action and constrain how that action can occur. After execution, it must establish what actually happened in the external world — and fail safely when the outcome is incomplete, ambiguous or contradictory.</p>
<p>Using examples from payments, procurement and infrastructure automation, we will examine where existing API and identity controls stop, what additional runtime evidence becomes necessary, and how enterprises can prevent autonomous execution from silently amplifying authority during retries, failures and recovery.</p>
<p>The goal is a practical architecture for letting AI agents act without giving them more authority than the enterprise intended.</p>

### Vulnerability Remediation: tackling on all fronts

<p>Every decision in life carries risk—whether it’s eating blue-rare steak, drinking tap water, or bathing in a pool maintained by an amateur. While every action has potential consequences, the actual danger depends on your circumstances, context, and frequency.</p>
<p>Software is no different. Using third-party libraries carries inherent risks, which amplify when a proven exploit exists. However, not all vulnerabilities represent a real-world threat.</p>
<p>In this talk I will present some of the challenges when dealing with Supply Chain Vulnerabilities at scale and we will also explore some strategies followed to provide a comprehensive set of proactive, reactive and contextual (AI-assisted) vulnerability remediation measures.</p>

### Your Domain Model Is Your API Spec — and Your First Prompt

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:00–11:25
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Emerging API Best Practices
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Dr. Annegret Junker

<p>Most teams have now tried handing an LLM a vague description and asking for an API. The results are familiar: plausible-looking endpoints, invented fields, events that don't match anything real, and bugs that take hours to trace. The easy reaction is to blame the model. That's the wrong angle. LLMs don't produce wrong APIs because they're not capable — they produce wrong APIs because they have no access to your domain.
The fix is something API teams can already recognize: a precise specification. This talk shows that the collaborative modeling practices used to build shared understanding — Domain Storytelling and EventStorming — double as a remarkably good way to derive that specification, across both styles of API. EventStorming's command/event/policy structure maps almost one-to-one onto an API surface: commands become synchronous REST operations, domain events become asynchronous channels and messages, and policies become the orchestration and business rules that connect them. Bounded contexts become clean service and product boundaries. The result is a paired OpenAPI and AsyncAPI specification that is, in effect, a machine-readable domain model.
Using CookWithUs, a recipe-sharing platform modeled and built across three iterations, we walk a concrete pipeline: model the domain, derive the synchronous REST surface and the event-driven async surface together, and split both specs by bounded context. We show exactly what a well-formed spec gives an LLM — and exactly where generation breaks down when the spec is incomplete, ambiguous, or missing its events. We show how a sharper Ubiquitous Language produces a measurably better OpenAPI document, a cleaner set of AsyncAPI channels, and a frontend the model can generate without inventing a single field.
The central claim is simple: every naming decision, every event you make explicit, every scoping choice travels all the way into the generated code. A spec is only as good as the language behind it — and async APIs in particular punish vague language, because an event nobody named precisely is an event nobody can consume reliably. This is not a talk about replacing API designers with AI. It's about what happens when rigorous API specification meets a powerful new tool, and why the modeling work always comes first.</p>

### Right to Repair vs. Intellectual Property: Hardware’s Second Life

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:25–11:50
- Room: Dragonnier 6
- Conference: GreenIO
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Carlyne Sevestre

<p>Europe's push to reshore hardware and extend device lifespans runs straight into Intellectual Property law. The Right to Repair Directive (2024/1799) requires manufacturers to offer repair and spare parts, while the EU design reform introduces a &quot;repair clause&quot; opening the market for visible spare parts. Yet refurbishers and resellers of branded hardware remain exposed: limits of trademark exhaustion, use of the original brand on repaired goods, counterfeiting claims. This talk maps where IP still blocks a genuine second life and how to build repair-friendly IP strategies.</p>

### AI writes the code, so who writes the proof? E2E testing in AI-assisted development

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 7
- Conference: Karate Labs 2nd User Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Pier-Jean Malindrino

### Autonomous Threat Detection at Scale: What Agentic AI Changes for Security Operations

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Willem Berroubache

<p>Security operations at telco scale generate a volume of events that no human team and no static ruleset can handle alone. At Orange, we decided to take a different path: building a fully on-premises, autonomous threat detection platform orchestrated by AI agents.</p>
<p>This talk shares what we've learned deploying this stack in a real production environment. We'll cover how we designed a multi-agent orchestration architecture where specialized agents handle ingestion, correlation, anomaly detection and response coordination in parallel, how we scaled ML inference on local GPU hardware, and how the whole system is structured to keep humans in the loop where it matters most.</p>
<p>We'll also go deep on the protocols and standards that made this work at scale: how we leverage MCP to give agents structured, contextual access to tools and data sources, and how A2A enables our agents to communicate, delegate and collaborate with each other in a way that's both auditable and extensible. These aren't just implementation details — they're what makes the difference between a fragile prototype and a production-grade agentic system.</p>
<p>One of the things that changed our perspective: the orchestrator's ability to adapt automatically to different operational contexts, whether it's 5G core network functions, fixed network infrastructure, or broader IT systems. Same platform, different threat landscapes, without rebuilding everything from scratch each time.</p>
<p>Because autonomy doesn't mean removing people from the equation. It means giving them better signals, at the right moment, so they can make faster and more confident decisions. The agents handle the noise. The humans handle the judgment calls.</p>
<p>We'll show how this shift changed the way our security teams operate day to day: less time chasing false positives, more time on what actually counts. And we'll be straight about what it takes to go from a promising PoC to something you'd trust in a production environment.</p>

### Build Fast, Run Safe: Agentic ML from First Edit to Production

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 6
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Michelle Hwang, Simeng Yang

<p>Coding agents can rewrite an ML pipeline in minutes. The harder problem is running those changes against real data and distributed compute without slow CI/CD for every experiment—or giving an unattended agent broader access than its human owner.</p>
<p>We show how we built an agent-ready Flyte platform on Kubernetes that overlays changed source files across a workflow's Python-project dependency graph. Engineers and agents can modify workflow orchestration, shared libraries, and model components together, then run the full pipeline remotely without rebuilding an image after every eligible change.</p>
<p>Lifecycle-aware skills guide agents through workflow creation, execution, failure diagnosis, and experiment comparison. A read-only MCP interface provides execution context, while persistent development environments enable unattended experiment loops under the requesting human's identity.</p>
<p>We then explain the security model that allows this workflow to scale across enterprise teams: trusted human-to-workload identity propagation, team-scoped permissions, separate production execution identities, and onboarding through existing directory groups.</p>
<p>We close with the hardening that makes these boundaries hold—approved images, scoped secrets, workload isolation, and reviewed production promotion. In the latest four-week period, successful top-level workflow executions reached more than 24,000—nearly triple the preceding four weeks. Attendees will leave with practical patterns for fast agentic experimentation without surrendering enterprise control.</p>

### Building a Self-Service Observability Platform with OpenTelemetry

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Imma Valls

<p>On an AI-native platform, humans are not the only ones who read and write telemetry. LLM applications and agents add new workloads to observe, and AI that investigates incidents and supports operational decisions also becomes a telemetry consumer.</p>
<p>This session shows how a platform team can build a common observability platform with OpenTelemetry as the shared language, so that development teams get consistent telemetry through self-service. It covers three building blocks: an SDK distribution with zero-code instrumentation, the OpenTelemetry Collector layer and its fleet management, and governance of semantic conventions. For each, it explains what developers set up themselves and what the platform controls. It then extends the same foundation to AI workloads with GenAI semantic conventions and to AI agents that participate in incident investigation.</p>

### Data Science : the multibillons dollars failures

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Marie Sacksick

<p>At a time where more and more budget is poured into AI, and the infamous rule of 80% data science projects which don't make it to production (or are cut early) is still relevant, let's investigate the why!
We’ve listed 126 public cases in which a data science project led to an economical disaster. In this talk, we want to have a look about the why these projects failed, how much they cost to the company, if the failure could have been avoided, and if so, how.
As failures that are public are necessarily huge scale, and with end-user impact, they do not cover the whole reality. It will be enriched with failures known by the speaker, either because they are personal, or acquaintances stories.</p>

### From click to terminal: API engineering in the age of agents

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Anthony Viard

<p>We can define API engineering as the set of practices that keep an API alive independently of the code that implements it: its contract, documentation, tests, monitoring, CI/CD, and so on.</p>
<p>Doing API engineering with agents means making them autonomous across that entire chain: reading a contract, analyzing risk, running tests, interpreting results. But without proper framing and tooling, an agent can easily drift or hallucinate, pulling us away from our goal. That risk is all the more real today, as an agent is already capable of producing and reviewing code on its own, in natural language.</p>
<p>This is why we need to design our tools not only for the developers who steer the agent, but for the agent itself.</p>
<p>In this talk, we'll look at how to improve &quot;&quot;Agent Experience&quot;&quot; by designing tools that an agent can understand and use on its own, then explore the work the teams did to bring the Postman CLI, the plugin, and the skills to the terminal. We'll then see how my Claude Code agent uses them to build an API from scratch and integrate it into CI/CD: running an analysis, setting up a quality gate that ensures the API stays understandable to an agent, or verifying that a change doesn't break anything elsewhere in the organization.</p>

### From expert judgments to LLM quality signals

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API for AI Governance
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Arianna Bonazza

<p>Large Language Models are increasingly used to support sensitive decisions in advertising, such as detecting greenwashing risks or assessing inclusive representation. But evaluating LLMs in these domains with classic data science is difficult: the “right answer” is rarely a single fact and often depends on expert interpretation. This creates a measurement gap—teams need quantitative signals to choose and improve models, while still respecting the qualitative nature of expert judgment, especially when labeled data is scarce.</p>
<p>We built the Positive AI LLM Arena, a Streamlit-based evaluation platform that turns expert review into a guided, scalable, and rigorous benchmark. By structuring the workflow and reducing reviewer effort, the Arena transforms data collection from a tedious task into a smoother (and even enjoyable) process, helping teams collect high-quality judgments at scale.</p>
<p>The key innovation is gamified, blind comparison. In the Analysis Arena, experts review an asset, replicate the reasoning expected from the LLM, label principles using operational categories, and then compare their decisions to anonymized, side-by-side outputs from different models. A lightweight progress scoring layer sustains consistent, high-standard reviews and produces continuous evaluation signals and decision-grade ground truth.</p>
<p>This talk explains how ML user experience patterns (gamification) and human-in-the-loop design can reduce bias, improve consistency, and generate reliable benchmarks that directly inform model selection, performance tracking, and reliability improvements for responsible communication use cases.</p>

### MCP: is it a new API paradigm? From a connectivity layer to a distribution strategy.

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: New Paradigms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Anna Maj

<p>Over 25 years in payments and fintech, I have seen connectivity evolve from proprietary integrations to open APIs—and become a strategic driver of new business models.</p>
<p>Open Banking enabled access to banking information and payment initiation.
Open Finance expanded the data opportunity to other financial services.
Embedded Finance brought financial services into other customer journeys, having enabled non-financial verticals.</p>
<p>Now, Model Context Protocol (MCP) adds another dimension: connecting financial data, tools and services to AI assistants and agents.
I see this as a new API paradigm: a standardised way for AI applications to discover and use capabilities exposed by financial providers, often through their existing APIs.</p>
<p>🎯  From a connectivity layer to a distribution channel - a key shift here.</p>

### Navigating the Agentic stack on Kubernetes: a layer-by-layer architectural guide

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Laurent Grangeau

<p>Running AI agents on Kubernetes requires rethinking cloud-native design. Unlike microservices, agentic workloads execute untrusted code, run multi-turn reasoning loops, and spend significant time idle—causing security risks, high latency, and wasted compute.</p>
<p>This session delivers a practical, layer-by-layer architectural blueprint for building a production agentic stack:</p>
<ul>
<li>Isolation &amp; Runtime Security: Protecting host kernels during dynamic code execution.</li>
<li>Ephemeral Warm-Pools: Eliminating cold starts with pre-allocated sandbox buffers.</li>
<li>Compute Density: Decoupling logical actors from physical pods via memory hibernation.</li>
<li>Durable Workflows: Managing state, event logs, and human-in-the-loop pauses.</li>
<li>Zero-Trust Identity &amp; Mesh: Securing tool calls and inter-agent delegation with short-lived tokens.</li>
</ul>
<p>Attendees will leave with a clear decision framework for designing each layer using cloud-native open-source patterns.</p>

### Platform Engineering for AI Agents: Creating Golden Paths for Autonomous Workloads

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Andreas Mouzong

<p>Over the last decade, Platform Engineering has focused on empowering human developers through self-service infrastructure, Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs), GitOps workflows, and Kubernetes-based golden paths.</p>
<p>As organizations adopt AI agents for coding, operations, support, and automation, a new challenge emerges: AI agents are becoming platform users too.</p>
<p>How do we provision, secure, govern, observe, and manage autonomous workloads at scale?</p>
<p>This talk explores the next evolution of Platform Engineering: building platforms designed not only for human developers, but also for AI agents operating in production environments.</p>
<p>Through a practical cloud-native architecture, we will demonstrate how Platform Engineering concepts such as GitOps, Backstage, Kubernetes, policy-as-code, observability, and Model Context Protocol (MCP) can be combined to create safe and scalable golden paths for autonomous workloads.</p>
<p>Attendees will learn why AI agents should be treated as first-class platform consumers and how platform teams can prepare for the emerging era of agent-enabled software delivery and operations.</p>

### Register Once, Integrate Everywhere: FAPI 2.0 and a Federated Authorization Foundation for Public-Sector APIs

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 1
- Conference: Europe API Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alexander Hoose

<p>Public-sector APIs are becoming critical infrastructure for digital governments. Yet in federated environments, services define their own authorization models, registration processes and security requirements. This leads to duplicated integration effort, inconsistent permission models and fragmented security across the public-sector API landscape.
The Federal API Authorization Infrastructure project, developed by Saxony-Anhalt and FITKO on behalf of the German IT Planning Council, addresses this challenge by defining uniform security requirements and a target architecture for federal API authorization.
The approach is based on established open standards such as OAuth 2.0, OpenID Connect, the FAPI 2.0 Security Profile and AuthZEN, rather than introducing a bespoke authorization scheme.
The talk presents the architectural vision, scope and key design decisions. We explain how the architecture structures API provision, integration and usage across federal base services, and how the Federal Platform Directory acts as a central self-service
component for registration, API-client onboarding and managing authorization attributes and policies.
We will discuss selected trade-offs, including sender-constrained tokens, client authentication, signed software statements, attribute-based authorization and the balance between centrally governed policies and decentralized execution and enforcement. The session also shows how reusable architecture artifacts, security requirements and migration paths support operators, developers and security teams in building more secure, interoperable and economically viable API ecosystems in
federated public administration.
All artifacts — security requirements, requirements documents, architecture decision records, and the target architecture — are published openly on OpenCode:
<a href="https://gitlab.opencode.de/sachsen-anhalt/mid/foederale-apiautorisierungsinfrastruktur/-/">https://gitlab.opencode.de/sachsen-anhalt/mid/foederale-apiautorisierungsinfrastruktur/-/</a>
blob/main/README.md</p>
