### The Hidden Complexity of Aggregated APIs: Lessons from Optimizing a Global Hotel Distribution Platform

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Nermine Reyadh

<p>APIs look clean on paper: structured contracts, predictable responses, elegant documentation. But when you aggregate dozens of third-party suppliers into a single global platform, the reality is far messier.</p>
<p>In this talk, I’ll share practical lessons from optimizing a large-scale hotel distribution API powering search, prebook, and booking flows across multiple suppliers and markets. Behind every “200 OK” lies a complex ecosystem of inconsistent rate structures, mapping mismatches, duplicate properties, timeout constraints, rate violations, and fragile upstream systems.</p>
<p>We’ll explore what really happens when you try to guarantee performance and reliability in an environment you don’t fully control. How do you handle supplier instability without degrading user experience? How do you design around strict booking SLAs (search → prebook → book) under heavy traffic? How do you balance latency, accuracy, cost, and margin?</p>
<p>This session is not about theory or API design patterns in isolation. It’s about operating aggregated APIs in the real world — where commercial impact, infrastructure constraints, and ecosystem chaos intersect.</p>
<p>If you work with third-party integrations at scale, this talk will give you practical frameworks and battle-tested insights you can apply immediately.</p>

### The Pod Is Reproducible. What About the Accelerator Underneath It?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 11:30–11:55
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Mauro Morales, Jasper De Keukelaere

<p>AI experiments increasingly depend on more than a model and a container. The accelerator underneath them brings kernels, drivers, firmware and hardware-specific operating-system requirements into the reproducibility boundary.</p>
<p>imec’s EdgeLab gives researchers access to a deliberately non-uniform pool of 10+ edge AI device types, including NVIDIA Jetsons. At build time, the system environment defines what each device needs: kernel, accelerator drivers, userspace and other hardware-specific requirements. At runtime, researchers request that hardware through pods on a shared Kubernetes cluster, run their experiments, and can discard and recreate the workload when it breaks.</p>
<p>That separation matters because these devices aren’t meant to stay in the lab. Edge hardware may sit in a datacenter while researchers experiment with it, but eventually workloads need to run in the field. If the system underneath the workload changes along the way, the environment being deployed is no longer the environment that was tested.</p>
<p>Containers solve only part of that problem. Below the kernel, edge hardware does not behave like a homogeneous datacenter fleet. BMCs and network boot cannot be assumed. Jetsons have their own flashing requirements. Different accelerators can require different kernels, drivers and userspace. Kubernetes begins once that machine exists; managing how it got there is a separate lifecycle.</p>
<p>This talk examines the resulting reproducibility problem. In EdgeLab, the reproducible unit extends beyond the container to include the system image that makes the accelerator usable. We will show how image-based machine management provides one operational model while still allowing hardware-specific images to differ deliberately, and how carrying that environment from lab to field can reduce the gap between what was tested and what is ultimately deployed.</p>
<p>Jasper De Keukelaere will show why imec built EdgeLab, how researchers use it, and what the lab can reproduce today. Mauro Morales will examine the machine lifecycle underneath it: system images, hardware-specific divergence, upgrades and rollback.</p>
<p>Finally, we’ll draw the boundary explicitly: which parts of an AI experiment can travel from lab to field today, which remain outside that boundary, and why knowing the difference matters when trying to reproduce an inference result.</p>

### Your project needs a bank account, not a foundation (yet)

### Boring Work Is Where Agents Actually Start Paying Rent

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Moritz Johner

<p>The best enterprise use case for agents is not glamorous. It is the work every engineer agrees matters, every team deprioritizes, and every security review eventually drags back into the room.</p>
<p>CVE remediation is the perfect example because the small-scale story is so seductive: bump the dependency, run CI, merge the PR and move on. That story collapses when you own thousands of repositories. Now the hard part is not the patch. It is finding the right artifact, proving the right fix, reconciling CI, containing the agent, and producing enough evidence and observability for people to trust the outcome.</p>
<p>This talk is a production case study of PatchPilot, an agentic remediation platform built in a regulated industry. I will show how we moved from prototype to production with controller loops, remediation agents, CI reconciliation, approval boundaries, egress controls, observability, and metrics that proved whether the system was creating value or just burning tokens.</p>
<p>The hard part was not getting an agent to open a pull request. The hard part was making it constrained enough for infosec and useful enough for engineers. This is the story of what failed, what changed, and why boring work might be the best place for agents to prove they belong.</p>

### Can Harness Engineering make Smaller AI Models Viable?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Naresh Jain

<p>As coding agents become more capable, the default response to difficult engineering tasks is often to reach for a more powerful model. But what if we could improve the environment around the model instead?</p>
<p>I built a behaviour harness for AI-driven API development using OpenAPI and AsyncAPI specifications, representative examples, contract testing, service virtualisation, API coverage, and deterministic feedback. The idea was simple: give a coding agent better guidance and faster feedback, and it should complete the task with fewer tokens and at lower cost.</p>
<p>The experiment produced the opposite result.</p>
<p>With a frontier coding model, the harness made the task nearly five times more expensive and twice as slow, with little difference in the final outcome. The model was already capable enough to solve the problem, and the harness became overhead.</p>
<p>But when I repeated the experiment with a substantially smaller model, the result changed. Without the harness, the model could not successfully complete the task. With the harness, it could, at approximately one-eighth the token cost of the unharnessed frontier model, although with some reduction in code quality.</p>
<p>This talk walks through the experiment, including the API development task, behaviour harness, isolated test environments, evaluation criteria, results, and limitations. More importantly, it explores a different way of thinking about AI-assisted software development: perhaps the purpose of a good harness isn’t to make an expensive model cheaper, but to make an expensive model unnecessary.</p>

### Context Is a Resource: How to Burn Fewer Tokens and Hit Zero Egress

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Weronika Kołodziej

<p>LLM agents running on Kubernetes reconstruct context from scratch on every turn, calling external APIs, injecting raw responses into prompts, and storing conversation history in pod memory. Practitioners recognize these as solved problems in distributed systems. In AI workloads, they are being ignored.</p>
<p>This session walks through a flight search agent to demonstrate four patterns: enforcing a token budget as a first-class resource with a hard token cap on tool results, measured in CI, using explicit tool-calling as the retrieval interface instead of sidecar injection, co-locating working memory inside the cluster to eliminate retrieval egress and replacing polling loops with push-based database subscriptions for event-driven state updates.</p>
<p>All cost numbers are measured, not estimated. Let’s see how low we can get with tokens per request. We will compare a huge naive baseline against near-zero retrieval egress traffic, and show five infrastructure components replaced by one.</p>

### Context management in large organisations : How to Make better AI decisions

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alex Hayem

<p>Most companies are not failing at AI because they lack powerful models. They are failing because even today’s leading enterprise AI tools — from Enterprise Copilot to GPT Enterprise — remain weak at reasoning over context.</p>
<p>When a user asks, “Have we done this before?”, most AI systems tend to answer in a binary way: yes or no. But in large organisations, the real question is rarely whether something happened before. It is whether the previous case was truly comparable: same type of counterparty, same risk level, same commercial upside, same legal constraints, same exception logic.</p>
<p>This is where context management becomes critical. Enterprise AI cannot simply retrieve documents through RAG and assume that similarity equals relevance. To understand context properly, organisations need more efficient models, but also a stronger interconnection of internal data points — across ERPs, CRMs, CLMs, document management systems, policies and knowledge bases. APIs play a central role here: they allow AI systems to access the right signals, from the right systems, at the right moment, to understand when a precedent should apply — and when it should not.</p>
<p>The issue is amplified by current AI user maturity. Employees often use short, direct prompts, which limits contextualisation. But overly complex prompts are not a reliable solution either: they increase the risk of hallucination and misleading outputs.</p>
<p>Drawing from my experience building API aggregation infrastructure at The Good Seat and internal contract memory at Mirmi AI, this talk will explore how large organisations can design better context systems so AI can support safer, more consistent decisions.</p>

### Continuous Expert-in-the-loop Agentic AI Validation

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Quincy Hsieh, Bertille Tierny, Muhammad Umer

<p>Unlike traditional software development, where output correctness can often be verified against predefined expected integration tests, validating an agentic AI requires sustained expert involvement to define what “good” means in context, to review ambiguous outputs, and to translate domain judgment into measurable evaluation criteria. Achieving this in practice, however, requires more than technical evaluation: enterprise deployment depends on aligning domain experts and AI engineers so that model choices reflect real business requirements. In many organizations, the gap between these two groups is where AI projects quietly fail when business accuracy diminishing by time. In this session, we introduce how Milliman streamline collaboration between domain experts and AI engineers, to accelerate validation cycles and to continuously improve the business accuracy from agents.</p>

### Deploying AI you can't see: patterns for privacy-preserving AI

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Guillaume Maron

<p>Most AI deployment assumes you can see the data you're working with. But what do you do when you cannot? When regulation, contractual commitments, or a zero-knowledge architecture mean the model must deliver intelligence without ever accessing raw user content?</p>
<p>This session presents a concrete engineering approach to that problem, drawn from building AI on top of a credential management system where all customer data is encrypted end-to-end.</p>
<p>We cover the full lifecycle: designing privacy boundaries before training begins; building training data sets and evaluation loops that do not rely on customer data; detecting distribution gaps without exposing sensitive inputs; improving models when debugging cannot involve raw data; and managing drift in production.</p>
<p>We then examine the infrastructure layer: how cloud secure enclaves and on-device inference each map to different risk profiles, latency requirements, and threat models.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with a playbook for treating privacy as a structural constraint from day one, and a framework for choosing the right inference infrastructure for systems that cannot afford to expose customer data.</p>

### Less Searching. More Shipping. Accelerating Kubernetes Operations with kagent

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Desislava Todorova

<p>Most of us have copied pod logs, events or YAML into ChatGPT or Claude at some point, hoping to get to the root cause faster. It works, but it also means constantly switching tools, providing context, and potentially exposing information that should never leave the cluster.</p>
<p>In this lightning talk, I’ll show how to bring an AI-powered Kubernetes agent into the cluster with kagent. Whether using a local model with Ollama or a hosted model like ChatGPT or Claude, kagent investigates issues using the cluster’s own context instead of relying on endless copy-pasting.</p>
<p>We’ll also look at Human-in-the-Loop and Agent-to-Agent - two concepts that make AI useful without giving up control - and introduce Khook, which can automatically trigger AI investigations when something goes wrong in the cluster. Along the way, we’ll discuss where these approaches shine, where they don’t, and how they can help platform teams spend less time troubleshooting and more time delivering software.</p>

### Open source's answer to CRA compliance and vulnerability chaos

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Dattier 2
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Philippe Ombredanne

<p>Vulnerability data is broken in the same way software supply chains were a decade ago: fragmented, duplicative, and impossible to trust without redoing the work yourself. The EU Cyber Resilience Act (CRA) is about to make that a legal problem, not just a technical one, as organizations are scrambling to prove they can track, report, and respond to vulnerabilities across their software supply chain. AboutCode is tackling this head-on with three interlocking efforts.</p>
<p>First, data: vulnerability information treated as a shared, curated and verified commons, deduplicated and traceable to source. This enables every team to stop re-triaging the same advisory from scratch all over again, and can produce the audit trail the CRA demands, backed by automation that correct data enables.</p>
<p>Second, standards: open, ecosystem-agnostic identifiers that let any tool, database, or organization refer to the same vulnerability the same way, closing a gap that has quietly wasted years of collective effort and complicated CRA reporting across fragmented toolchains. This means PURL and VERS, and a new identifier for security advisories.</p>
<p>Third, tooling: practical and trustworthy open source software that turns CRA and other regulatory compliance obligations from a manual paperwork burden into an automatable pipeline anyone can adopt.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with a clear framework for evaluating their own security data supply chain and meeting CRA obligations, and a new foundation for software supply chain security, using data you can trust, standards you can share, and tools you can run.</p>

- Date: 3 December 2026

### The Automation Paradox

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: New Paradigms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kate Astrid

<p>When AI does the easy work, humans get the hard parts. Automation removes repetitive tasks, but it also changes the skills humans need. As AI takes over coding, analysis, writing, and decision-making, we're increasingly left with ambiguity, exceptions, judgment, and responsibility.</p>
<p>This talk explores what automation does to human cognition and expertise. What happens when we stop practicing skills because machines do them for us? Why do we trust automated answers? And as AI becomes better at producing answers, does human expertise become more or less valuable?</p>

### The Organizational Data Maturity Score: An Open Source Framework for AI-Ready Data Foundation

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 6
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Evangelista Tragni, Dimedrik Feudjieu

<p>Every organisation wants AI. Very few know whether their data can survive it. Before a single model reaches production, the real blocker is almost always the same: no source cartography, no lineage, no way to prove, to a regulator or to yourself, where a number came from.</p>
<p>This talk introduces ODMS, the Organizational Data Maturity Score, an open source framework Devoteam drives with customers, and it's already running in production at regulated customers in Luxembourg (not a theory, a reference).</p>
<p>ODMS scores an organisation's data capability across 8 dimensions, from source cartography to measured business value, assessed against the existing state with a full open source software stack.</p>
<p>Each dimension is then read through the DORA, EU AI Act, and Solvency II lens, showing exactly where auditability already holds and where the evidence would collapse under scrutiny.</p>
<p>We'll walk through the real case: an air-gapped, sovereign lakehouse built entirely on open source foundations (Kubernetes, open table formats, open governance tooling) with roadmap to improve the DORA score.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with the 8-dimension maturity model itself, a way to translate &quot;we have a data problem&quot; into a funded, sequenced roadmap, and a concrete example of digital sovereignty applied to data, not infrastructure, for regulated environments where the audit trail is the product.</p>

### Your Own Kubernetes Cluster Without Vendor Lock-in

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:00–12:25
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Ralph Hees

<p>Kubernetes promises portability, and most clusters are less portable than their owners think. The workloads move easily. The managed database, load balancer, storage classes, identity system, secret store and backups tie you to one cloud. When prices rise or data sovereignty rules change, the migration takes months.</p>
<p>It does not have to be this way. Build the cluster on open-source components and describe everything in Git, and the provider becomes a replaceable layer that delivers nodes, storage and a network. This talk shows how to create and manage such a cluster with a small team and how to move it between providers, with examples from production workloads on European cloud providers.</p>
<p>We show
• Where lock-in hides in a Kubernetes setup, and how to recognize it early.
• How to describe the whole cluster in Git with Argo CD.
• How to run PostgreSQL, MariaDB and Valkey in the cluster with operators.
• How to keep secrets, identity and backups provider-neutral with External Secrets Operator, Keycloak and Velero.
• A walkthrough of a provider migration: what moved in hours and what took longer.</p>
<p>Key takeaways
• Kubernetes itself does not prevent lock-in. Your choice of surrounding services does.
• If your cluster is in Git, a new cluster is a commit away.
• Data is the hard part of every migration. Test your restore before you need it.</p>

### What broke when I left the vibe-coding platform

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:10–12:30
- Room: Dragonnier 3
- Conference: Build It Summit Paris 2026
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kim Rowden

<p>I've built two AI products, LaunchDocs and Query Trenches, one without writing the code myself. I'm a former healthcare professional turned application security trainer, working a full-time job, with an AI coding platform as my entire engineering team. It worked, until I needed to own what I had built. When I exported the code and audited it, most of what looked finished on screen wasn’t. Features half-wired, no scheduler, no way to delete user data, and AI calls quietly running through US servers despite my EU-first promise. This fireside chat is the honest account of moving out. It covers rebuilding on my stack with Claude Code, switching to Mistral to keep AI processing in Europe, deciding what to rebuild versus keep, and what I would do differently on day one. This is for anyone shipping with AI tools who wants to know what’s really under the hood before their customers find out.</p>

### EU Open Source Strategy: key role of the Open Internet Stack

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:15–12:25
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack

- Speakers: Ragnar Bergstrom

<p>In June 2026, the European Commission released the first ever EU wide Open Source Strategy (OSS), underlining the central role of open source in achieving EU technological sovereignty – defined as its capacity to develop, control and scale the technologies underpinning its economy and society, while remaining open to cooperation. \n\nAgainst the backdrop of the EU Tech Sovereignty Package, the Open Source Strategy will be presented and its full lifecycle approach with the ambition to grow the entire EU open source ecosystem: developing and adopting building blocks, scaling European businesses, strengthening stewardship, securing and maintaining critical software, and mobilising public administrations. \n\n The Open Internet Stack is a key part of the OSS, aimed at building an open, interoperable, sovereign technology stack—spanning hardware, middleware, connectivity, trust technologies, and decentralised systems.</p>

### ELFA: working on a local-first end-to-end encrypted office workspace

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:25–12:30

- Conference: SUPERstack

- Speakers: Niko Bonnieure

<p>We are building an end-to-end encrypted collaborative workspace, a suite of apps with productivity tools (text editor, spreadsheet, calendar, contacts, email, tables, forms, etc...) and a private social network. It is based on NextGraph, and it is local-first, which means it also works offline, and it synchronizes, without conflicts, when you have internet again. It is open source of course, and runs on an open platform, a federation that anybody can join, by self-hosting. This suite, called ELFA, is extensible, via an app store. We will dive briefly on what Local-First brings in terms of features, and how it combines with end-to-end-encryption.</p>

### CryptPad: a secure and privacy-friendly collaborative office suite

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:45
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Fabrice Mouhartem

<p>CryptPad is an European and Open-Source end-to-end encrypted collaborative office suite. It proposes in a web application, not only the usual office applications: spreadsheet, text processor, presentations, but also other tools such as privacy-friendly forms, kanbans for light project management, calendars, and more in an interface as familiar as possible.</p>
<p>The main difference between CryptPad and other solution is its integration of cryptography from login to document editing. By doing this, it ensures the security of the collaboration against eavesdroppers (including the server hosting the data). In the case of sensitive documents that has to be stored in your own infrastructure, for which people from your infrastructure management should not have access to, it offers the best degree of confidentiality you can afford.</p>
<p>However, CryptPad lacks interoperability for the moment. If it’s possible to export your document (and thus be responsible of the confidentiality of the document once decrypted and stored locally), it’s not possible yet to collaborate and exchange documents between CryptPad instances. This issue is to be addressed within the SuperStack consortium which aims at providing a fully-fledged ecosystem of open-source applications that work together.</p>
<p>In this talk, I will present the collaborative office suite CryptPad and how it works, and the future developments within the SuperStack consortium.</p>

The site went from 100 to 1,500+ pages in 4 languages, and the agency bill from €3.2k a month to zero. This session covers the stack, the brief-to-page workflow and the mistakes to avoid.</p>

### Why MCP Needs an OpenAPI Moment

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:50
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Stève Sfartz

<p>The MCP protocol standardizes how clients and servers communicate at runtime. But how do we reason about an MCP server before we implement it, deploy it, or connect an agent to it?</p>
<p>The API ecosystem went through a similar transition. OpenAPI made APIs reviewable, documentable, testable, lintable, and governable before runtime. MCP now has an opportunity to establish a comparable design-time layer.</p>
<p>This talk explores what a machine-readable MCP server description could unlock: design-first workflows, generated documentation, quality rules, contract reviews, change detection, code generation, and governance.</p>
<p>We will use mcpdesc, an open specification for describing MCP server interfaces, published through the mcpdesc.org initiative, as a concrete exploration of what such a design-time layer could look like.</p>

### Agentic Paved Roads: Dev Velocity meets Istio Zero-Trust Ops

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Mofesola Babalola, Hannah Olukoye

<p>When rolling out agentic AI and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers across an enterprise, a natural tension emerges. Engineering Managers want velocity. They push to integrate multi-agent swarms with internal APIs to accelerate feature delivery. Meanwhile, Infrastructure Engineers are staring down an operational nightmare. Rogue agents hammer internal databases, token spend becomes unpredictable, and massive new attack vectors bypass traditional network perimeters.</p>
<p>In this dual-perspective session, an Engineering Manager and a Lead Infrastructure Engineer will debate and dissect the &quot;Day 2&quot; realities of scaling agentic systems. We will show how to resolve the velocity versus control paradox by building an Agentic Paved Road powered by Istio Ambient Mesh.</p>
<p>Together, we will demonstrate how to leverage Istio and eBPF to enforce zero-trust security on agent-to-agent traffic without the dreaded sidecar tax that starves GPU nodes of memory. The Engineering Manager will cover managing agentic debt, tracking ROI, and developer enablement. The Infrastructure Engineer will dive into the technical implementation. We will show how to deploy node-level ztunnels and L7 Waypoint proxies alongside the new Gateway API Inference Extension. You will learn how to rate-limit, audit, and secure MCP tool calls entirely at the platform layer, ensuring intelligent LLM inference routing without slowing down the development teams.</p>

### Less Status, More Signal: How AI Agents Changed Delivery Management

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alexis Revue

<p>Most delivery teams do not lack information. They lack usable signal.</p>
<p>We built an AI agent platform specialized in delivery management, adapted to our context and connected to our everyday tools. The ambition was simple: reduce the coordination burden on Delivery Managers by delegating repetitive work such as status collection, meeting preparation, action tracking, risk detection, dependency visibility, and project context retrieval.</p>
<p>But the real lesson was sharper: AI agents do not just improve delivery management; they expose it. They reveal where reporting creates noise, where rituals lack decisions, where ownership is unclear, and where teams confuse visibility with control.</p>
<p>This talk is a candid field report on what changed when we asked agents to take over a significant part of delivery operations. I will share where they created leverage, where they failed, and what we had to rethink to turn delivery noise into signal without outsourcing judgment, trust, or accountability.</p>

### Minion-Driven Development: Deploying a Fleet of AI Agents

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Henrik Rexed

<p>One Minion doing everything solo sounds efficient until it drowns in context and starts hallucinating migrations for the wrong database. You wouldn't send Kevin alone to steal the moon. You need a crew.</p>
<p>BMAD was my answer: specialized Minions, each with a focused role and a clean artifact-based handoff to the next. No Minion inherits the full project history, only what it needs. It worked. Except the OpenTelemetry instrumentation was a mess: stale SDKs, broken trace propagation, wrong semantic conventions. So I added a dedicated Observability Engineer to the crew.</p>
<p>Then I brought in OpenClaw as the boss Minion. I describe the goal, it drives the whole BMAD crew. Felt like Gru finally having a plan. Until I realized I was burning tokens just asking &quot;what's the status?&quot; I had zero visibility. Even Minions need supervision.</p>
<p>That frustration led me to Paperclip, a framework that manages agents like a real organization: tickets, assignments, audit trail. I built the BMAD crew for Paperclip so my Minions work with real structure. Now they check out tickets, escalate blockers, and I can see exactly what the crew is up to.</p>

### Signal Over Noise: Context Engineering as the Architecture Discipline Behind Agentic Enterprise AI

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: New Paradigms
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Matthias Biehl

<p>Your AI investments are delivering results but not consistently, and not at the scale you expected. The gap between the demo and production almost always comes down to the same root cause: the model doesn't know enough about your business at the moment it needs to act.</p>
<p>Context engineering, the practice of designing what enterprise data an AI system has access to, when, and in what form is the most important and least understood discipline in enterprise AI today. The teams quietly building reliable AI systems are winning by getting the right enterprise data in front of the model at the right moment.</p>
<p>In this session, we'll break down what context engineering actually is, why it's an architecture decision and not a prompting technique, and how to think about the two fundamental patterns, static injected context and dynamic live retrieval via tools like MCP. You'll leave with a clear mental model for diagnosing and building context for your enterprise  that holds up in production.</p>

### Sovereign by Design: Running Agent and Inference Traffic on a Control Plane You Own

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 6
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Hugo Guerrero, Tom O'Rourke

<p>The agent-orchestrated enterprise is fragile the moment both the reasoning and the execution live in someone else's cloud — and for European platform teams operating under real data-sovereignty, multi-region, and air-gapped constraints, that's not a hypothetical, it's a procurement requirement.</p>
<p>I make the case for a control plane you actually operate: MCP tool calls, A2A traffic, and LLM inference routed through open gateway and mesh infrastructure running on your own clusters, not a vendor's black box. agentgateway (a Linux Foundation project) federates MCP servers, applies JWT/OAuth, and deploys as ingress, waypoint, or egress; kgateway carries the same Gateway API path you already trust for cloud connectivity. Together they keep agent identity, routing, and audit inside infrastructure you control — including the disconnected and multi-region deployments sovereignty requirements actually demand.</p>
<p>Not a compliance checklist or a product pitch: the architecture case for why retrofitting a legacy proxy doesn't work for MCP/A2A, and what a genuinely sovereign agent stack looks like when you build it.</p>

### The Risks That Come with Underfunded API Standards

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Thulisile Sibanda

<p>The technology industry is investing heavily in AI, accelerating software development and relying on increasingly interconnected systems. Beneath this rapid evolution lies shared digital infrastructure: the open standards, specifications, and APIs that enable software ecosystems to communicate, innovate, and scale. However, the resources required to sustain this critical infrastructure are failing to keep pace with industry adoption.</p>
<p>When funding is uncertain, and only a few people remain to maintain these standards, the problem becomes more than financial; it becomes a security risk. Without enough support, API specifications go unfunded, security patches are delayed, and governance is overlooked. This makes software supply chains more vulnerable, especially as businesses rely on these systems more than ever.</p>
<p>Drawing on real-world community insights from the AsyncAPI initiative and the broader open-source ecosystem, this session challenges the assumption that shared technical infrastructure will remain resilient simply because it is open. We will explore the friction between massive commercial adoption and maintainer capacity, examine the cybersecurity risks of neglected open specifications, and discuss actionable models for long-term sustainability, governance, and collective industry stewardship.</p>

### The Unmanaged Node: Policy and Audit for AI Coding Agents

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Harald Nezbeda

<p>AI coding agents run on developer machines with full credentials. They execute shell commands, install packages, read every file in the project, and call external APIs. Your platform has network policy, workload identity, scoped secrets, and an audit trail — none of it reaches that machine. A public archive of 58 sourced incidents shows what goes wrong: deleted volumes, dropped production databases, leaked .env files, malicious packages installed on request. How do you put these agents back under policy?</p>
<p>This talk shows a practical approach, built on the observation that everything an agent does that you can't see leaves the machine over HTTP. That's where the control belongs. I'll show how an intercepting proxy enforces allow/deny rules per container rather than globally, why the client-supplied Host header can't drive that decision, when the filter should fail open and when it must fail closed, and how a bypass in our own policy parser was caught in review.</p>
<p>The same proxy produces the evidence. Every request lands in a local SQLite database with its host, source container, filter mode, block reason, and token spend, with credentials redacted before the write. I'll demonstrate the whole thing live using VibePod, an open source implementation.</p>

### Twake.ai: when the Sovereign Digital Workspace becomes Agentic

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Dragonnier 4
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Michel-Marie Maudet

<p>For last years, the &quot;European alternative to the US digital workplace&quot; was a slide in a presentation, not a product you could install. That has changed.</p>
<p>Twake.ai is an open source, AI-first collaborative suite: mail, chat, drive, documents and meetings. It now runs in production at a scale that answers the question. In France, ANFSI uses it for the internal security forces. The governments of Mauritius and Mongolia have adopted it as national infrastructure. And through LINAGORA's open source framework agreement with CANUT, every French local authority, health service, school can now order it and migrate through a standard public purchasing channel. This removes the procurement barrier that stopped most sovereignty projects before they even started.</p>
<p>The second half of the talk looks forward. We are making Twake.ai agentic. Agents work across mail, documents, tasks and business systems inside the customer's own tenant, under the organization's own rules, on European models. This is not one more AI assistant in your inbox. It is a move from the augmented user to the orchestrated organization, and it is the first time a sovereign stack can lead instead of follow.</p>
<p>I will share real numbers, real limits, and what we provide today to our customers.</p>

### Two Architects, One Mission: Rethinking How Systems Talk

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Fabrizio Lazzaretti

<p>Integration projects fail. Not because the technology doesn't work—it almost always does. They fail because communication patterns are picked based on habit rather than need. &quot;&quot;What did we use last time? REST? Great, let's do that again.&quot;&quot; These reflexes and other influences lead to the same issue: systems that may technically function but fundamentally misunderstand the business requirements. This leads to operational problems, incorrect outcomes, scaling issues, high costs, and migration efforts.</p>
<p>We've spent years helping organizations untangle these messes across industries. The pattern is always the same: a mismatch between what the business needs from an interaction and what the technical implementation delivers. A synchronous call that would benefit from eventual consistency for resilience. An event stream where a simple request-response would suffice. Fire-and-forget where guaranteed delivery matters.</p>
<p>In this session, we'll share what usually goes wrong when teams default to familiar patterns without examining requirements. We'll take you on a tour through the landscape of communication styles—synchronous and asynchronous, commands and events, orchestration and choreography—not as abstract theory but as tools with specific trade-offs. Most importantly, we'll give you a practical framework to diagnose what your current integration scenario actually demands.</p>
<p>You'll leave with a decision model that starts with business intent rather than technology preference. Because the goal isn't to use Kafka everywhere—or REST everywhere—but to choose the pattern that makes your systems genuinely talk the way your business needs them to.</p>

### Two Standards Walk Into an Agent: Runtime Agent Control with OpenTelemetry and OpenFeature

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Simon Schrottner

<p>Autonomous agents are being deployed without runtime control. No open, vendor-neutral way to change what they do. No visibility into why they decided. We can fix that. A physical party game. A Raspberry Pi. An agent that adapts and evolves the game in real time. Without a single proprietary platform.</p>
<p>Sounds fun. But it's also the argument. We've been here before.</p>
<p>Every vendor built their own observability stack. We paid the price - so we built OpenTelemetry. Same with feature flags - we built OpenFeature.</p>
<p>Now agents are here. The control layer is emerging again, often as vendor-specific infrastructure.</p>
<p>Nobody designed OpenTelemetry to be an agent's eyes. Nobody designed OpenFeature to be its conscience. Turns out they work anyway: OpenTelemetry becomes observation, OpenFeature becomes control.</p>
<p>This talk explores agent control on open standards — physical system control, infrastructure management, live model switching, permission changes, and rollback without redeployment.</p>
<p>Every autonomous agent needs a control layer. Vendors are racing to own it. You already have it.</p>

### Veeam & Proxmox

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Dattier 1
- Conference: ProxMox Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: René-François Mennecier

### Watts Matter: Power- and Carbon-Aware Scheduling for the AI-Era Data Center

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alessandro Stefouli-Vozza

<p>The next bottleneck in your data center isn't compute. It's the power feed.</p>
<p>As GPU clusters multiply, energy is becoming the scarcest resource in the AI-era data center — and the one Kubernetes knows least about. The scheduler that packs pods by CPU and memory is blind to watts, heat, and carbon.</p>
<p>This talk makes energy a first-class scheduling signal. Using open-source tooling, we'll measure per-workload power draw with Kepler, shift batch and training jobs to cheaper, cleaner hours with Kueue and KEDA, and pack GPUs for maximum utilization-per-watt.</p>
<p>You'll learn how to measure what every pod actually costs in watts, schedule around power caps, prices, and carbon intensity, raise GPU utilization instead of buying more GPUs, and avoid the failure modes: thermal throttling and starving batch queues.</p>
<p>Same clusters, same workloads — just running where and when the watts are right.</p>

### What Keeps You from Moving to PostgreSQL?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:30–12:55
- Room: Dattier 2
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Etienne Bersac

<p>Whatever is the tech revolution of the day, data is critical.
Moving to PostgreSQL lets you regain full ownership of your data while improving cost efficiency, security, features and performance.</p>
<p>Migration tools and methods have matured significantly, making the transition more accessible than ever, both technically and financially.
This session explores why it’s time to reconsider moving to PostgreSQL.</p>

### Sustainable Digital Value Chains: How Enterprise Architecture Enables IT Sustainability at Vodafone

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 12:40–13:00
- Room: Davidia 1
- Conference: GreenIO
- Type: Workshop
- Speakers: Rainer Scheibehenne

<p>As sustainability becomes a key differentiator in digital markets, IT sustainability at Vodafone is evolving from a compliance obligation to a strategic value driver. This session shows how Enterprise Architecture creates transparency across digital value chains and links IT assets to their environmental impact. We share insights from an ongoing transformation and demonstrate how EA supports data-driven sustainability management.</p>

<p>Our digital world has no off switch: modern societies depend on software and digital infrastructure. Yet its energy and environmental footprint keeps growing. That is the No Exit Dilemma. ### An open business capability map for APIs and Enterprise Architecture

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Enterprise Architecture for the Agentic Era
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Vincent Verdet

<p>A business capability map gives enterprise architects one shared language, from the board's heatmap down to the application inventory. It does the same for APIs : API catalogues are organised by supply: by team, gateway or endpoint. The business, and the AI agents acting for it, look by need. Yet most capability reference models are proprietary, and many EA functions still start from a blank page.</p>
<p>This talk presents an open alternative: the Turbo EA capability catalogue, free under CC BY 4.0. It holds more than 9,600 capabilities across 27 industries, plus 1,300+ processes and 65 end-to-end value streams, in eight languages with stable IDs.</p>
<p>Then the part I find most interesting: how it was built. The question was simple : could AI build a capability model from the ground up ? From defining the governance model to validating a multi-step approach, I will show how to use a non-deterministic model to produce near-deterministic output, industry after industry.</p>

### Building usage-based billing for banks

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Hassane Moustapha

<p>Banks already charge for everything: transfers, card payments, statements, API calls. The pricing logic usually lives in spreadsheets, in core banking parameters, and in people's heads. When a regulator changes a rule, it
takes months to follow.</p>
<p>My team and I built a platform to fix this. It's a usage-based billing platform: every event comes in through an API, gets metered, gets rated against a pricing plan, and ends up on an invoice. It's designed for banks and their
subsidiaries.</p>
<p>In this talk I'll walk through the real architecture and the problems that come with it:</p>
<ul>
<li>idempotent ingestion</li>
<li>keeping pricing calculations consistent across services</li>
<li>re-billing from a group to its subsidiaries</li>
<li>adapting to regulatory pricing changes without a rewrite.</li>
</ul>

### DRA Allocates, But Who Enforces? Closing the GPU Sharing Gap with HAMi

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Ivan Porta

<p>With Kubernetes v1.36, DRA's partitionable-devices and consumable-capacity features reached beta and are now enabled by default. This means pods can request part of a GPU, and the scheduler will not overcommit a device's capacity. But does this solve the problem of idle GPU usage? Not quite. DRA handles allocation, but not enforcement during runtime. After a pod starts, the Kubernetes API does not stop a process from using more than its assigned share. Enforcing limits is still up to the driver, which today means using MIG's fixed hardware partitions or, if using time-slicing, having almost no enforcement.</p>
<p>In this session, we'll take a practical look at HAMi, the CNCF Incubating GPU virtualization middleware that addresses the enforcement gap in software. We'll trace a shared GPU request from start to finish, covering the device plugin, mutating webhook, scheduler extender, and the in-container library that intercepts CUDA calls to keep workloads within their assigned slice. Finally, we'll also see how HAMi works alongside DRA using its own DRA driver, rather than competing with it.</p>

### Digital Workplace : What European (real) alternatives are there?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Dragonnier 4
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Denis Lafont-Trévisan

### Don't Blame the Agent. It Only Believed Your API Contract

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API for AI Governance
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Deepti Mittal

<p>Every REST API we've ever shipped carries assumptions it never wrote down. That the consumer read the documentation. That when something fails, it will eventually get tired and stop. That an error message is prose for a person. That &quot;try again later&quot; is a complete answer. That anyone holding a valid token is entitled to act on it.
Those assumptions held for fifteen years, because on the other end was a developer with a browser tab open and a finite supply of patience. None of them survive an autonomous consumer.</p>
<p>This is a code-first talk, not a tour of what agents are.   This talks covers how to write discoverable capabilities instead of a README nobody opens, errors as control flow instead of apology text, mutations that survive being retried, an explicit contract for &quot;not yet,&quot; and an authority boundary for the actions that are too expensive to take unilaterally.
Audience will  leave with the five questions to ask in  next design review — and a much clearer sense of whose fault the next incident actually is.</p>

### From Seven Silos to One Question: MCP in Production for Hydrogen Aircraft Design

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Abdoulaye Balde, Jacques-Alexis Verrecchia

<p>Designing a hydrogen-electric aircraft means answering questions that cross every tool boundary an engineering team owns. &quot;Can we fly Paris to Geneva, and what does that do to our requirement baseline?&quot; touches a 7.6 GB PostgreSQL database of real flight history, a PyTorch mission simulator, and a requirements SaaS — three systems that share no protocol, no auth model, and no vocabulary. Today the engineer opens all three by hand, and MATLAB models and the PLM are next in line.</p>
<p>We built the layer that lets one agent answer instead, and it has been in production at Beyond Aero since July. The unit of composition is the Model Context Protocol server: one use case = one MCP server = N namespaced tool groups, published to a registry, executed through a single gateway, and switched on per user from a catalogue page — with no redeploy.</p>
<p>This talk is the architecture and the scars, in equal measure. How a registry (what exists) and a gateway (how you call it) stay consistent when your cloud provider links neither — the namespace is the only rope, and nothing enforces it. Why a cross-region tool target is rejected outright. Why a single 64-character tool name fails the entire model call rather than just that tool. Why tools/list is paginated and what breaks when you ignore that. And what to do when a vendor's own MCP server refuses the handshake over one unimplemented capability method: we ended up authoring theirs, generating 44 read-only tools from their OpenAPI and cutting schema cost from 19.6k to 5.3k tokens.</p>
<p>You will leave with a composition model for heterogeneous engineering tooling, the failure modes we hit in production with dates and measurements attached, and the three things we would wire differently.</p>

### The Uncomfortable Truth About Enterprise AI: Tech Is Easy, Governance Is Hard

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Paula Oses

<p>Most discussions about enterprise AI focus on models, infrastructure, and adoption. Our experience was different. Creative Studio was built within IAG Transform AI by a multidisciplinary team of creatives and technologists operating with a startup-style model inside one of the world's largest airline groups. The objective seemed straightforward: provide secure access to Generative AI capabilities for British Airways, Iberia, Vueling, LEVEL, Aer Lingus and IAG Loyalty while keeping enterprise data, brand assets and intellectual property protected.</p>
<p>The technology challenge turned out to be the easy part. The real challenge was designing an operating model capable of balancing innovation and control across multiple brands, business units and risk profiles. As adoption expanded, we discovered that governance could not be implemented as a layer on top of the platform. It had to become part of the architecture itself.</p>
<p>In this session, we will share the technical and operational lessons learned from running Generative AI in production inside a regulated enterprise environment. We will discuss how we approached identity and access management, content provenance, auditability, intellectual property protection through image similarity controls, EU AI Act considerations, approval workflows, and role-based guardrails driven by risk rather than job title. We will also explore one of our biggest lessons learned: not every user should have access to the same AI capabilities, and not every piece of content should follow the same governance path. Using real examples from supporting six operating companies, we will explain how governance decisions influenced platform architecture, permission models, content workflows and operational processes. Attendees will leave with practical patterns for building AI platforms where security, governance and compliance are not afterthoughts, but foundational design principles.</p>

### Your AI Agent Is Only as Good as Its Schema: Designing Reliable MCP Tools with JSON Schema

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:00–14:25
- Room: Aubépine 5
- Conference: API Standards Conference
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Ayodele Aransiola

<p>AI agents are becoming active consumers of APIs, but many of the contracts they rely on were designed only for traditional applications and human developers. That creates ambiguity, unreliable tool calls, inconsistent outputs, and unnecessary complexity when teams introduce MCP or other agent-based systems.</p>
<p>This talk explores why JSON Schema is becoming critical infrastructure for reliable AI tooling. Using MCP tools as a practical example, I’ll show how well-designed schemas can make agent interactions more predictable, improve validation, reduce ambiguity, and create clearer contracts between models and backend systems.</p>
<p>The goal is to move the conversation beyond prompts and model choice, and focus on something more fundamental: if an agent cannot clearly understand what a tool accepts and returns, the quality of the model alone will not make the system reliable.</p>

### Yivi a wallet Europe can actually own

- Date: 3 December 2026

But if those wallets depend entirely on Google and Apple, Europe’s digital identity runs on someone else’s terms. This talk gives a short overview of the legislation and makes the case for open, sovereign wallets that can ship outside the big-tech ecosystems, based on lessons from building Yivi.</p>

### Agentic factories in production: running AI agents that write 100% of the code

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Acacia 4
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Quentin Pleplé

<p>Not a single line of code typed by hand. For Pernod Ricard, we rewrote the application that manages the marketing budgets of the whole group, a 10-year-old AngularJS monolith, into NestJS and React in 7 months. An agentic factory produced all of the code.</p>
<p>Agents are a new kind of workload: they run day and night and never behave the same way twice. This field report shows how we run them in production: specialized agents taking turns in a fixed loop (reverse engineer, plan, implement, validate, kaizen), every pull request tested for equivalence with the legacy system in CI/CD, agent instructions versioned alongside the code, and humans placed only at the checkpoints that matter.</p>
<p>We went up to 10x faster per migrated feature, and quality went up instead of down. I will also share what breaks, what we still review and what we no longer review, and what the developer's job becomes when the team steers a pipeline that codes on its own.</p>

### Beyond CI/CD: Engineering Agentic Release Operations at Enterprise Scale

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kai Levin

<p>CI/CD has transformed how we build, test, and deploy software. Yet in large enterprises, product releases still involve extensive manual coordination across fragmented engineering systems, approval processes, and compliance requirements.</p>
<p>What if AI agents could take ownership of this operational complexity without replacing the existing CI/CD infrastructure?</p>
<p>At Ericsson's Application Development Platform (ADP), we are exploring this shift through an AI-native Release Agent that orchestrates activities across the product release lifecycle. Already adopted in production workflows across 11 ADP services, participating teams have reported 50–80% reductions in manual release-handling effort for supported workflows.</p>
<p>This session takes a practical look at the architecture and engineering challenges of introducing agentic AI into established cloud-native operations.</p>
<p>Drawing on our experience at Ericsson, we will explore:</p>
<ul>
<li>Beyond deterministic pipelines: Where traditional automation ends and agentic orchestration begins.</li>
<li>Agents across fragmented systems: Using agent capabilities, skills, and Model Context Protocol (MCP) integrations to coordinate existing engineering infrastructure.</li>
<li>Designing for operational autonomy: Progressing from human-approved orchestration toward supervised and policy-governed autonomous execution.</li>
<li>Engineering for trust and reliability: Managing execution state, human approval boundaries, observability, failures, and governance.</li>
<li>Lessons from real-world adoption: What we have achieved, the challenges encountered, and what remains to be solved.</li>
</ul>
<p>Rather than presenting autonomous operations as a finished solution, we will examine the practical transition from isolated AI assistants to increasingly autonomous operational workflows.</p>
<p>Attendees will leave with architectural considerations and actionable lessons for introducing agentic AI into their own cloud-native platforms, building on the infrastructure and automation they already have.</p>

### Designing the Sovereign Agent Stack: MCP, A2A, and API Governance for the Agentic Era

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Baobab 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Enterprise Architecture for the Agentic Era
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jon Scheele

<p>Agentic AI raises a sovereignty problem: how do you let autonomous agents discover and act on your systems without losing control over data residency, auditability, and vendor lock-in? MCP and A2A give architects the plumbing, but not the governance model. This session maps the emerging protocol stack against the EU AI Act, NIST's AI Risk Management Framework, and Singapore's MAS model, translating three regulatory approaches into one practical architecture for building agent systems that are sovereign and governed by design, not by afterthought.</p>
<p>Key takeaways:
• Where MCP and A2A each fit in a sovereign agent stack, and why most enterprises need both
• How EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, and MAS requirements compare — and where they converge
• Governance patterns for agent-to-agent coordination that preserve auditability and data residency</p>

### Engineering Multiplayer API Sandboxes for Humans and Agents

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Nithin Kumar B

<p>Many multiplayer apps sync edits with a CRDT, a data structure that merges changes. That works for text or most design tools. An API schema, be it OpenAPI, gRPC or GraphQL, has rules, and two edits that are each fine can break it together.</p>
<p>We learned this building API sandboxes, private copies of an API that people and AI agents edit together, on schemas with over 200 services. So we built our own sync engine. Every edit, from a person or an agent, gets a clear answer on whether it worked.</p>
<p>Large schemas also outgrew a regular web canvas, so we built one that draws on the GPU. The canvas is where people follow an agent at work. It brings changes from agents into view, and describes itself to agents in return.</p>
<p>You don’t need to know CRDTs or graphics to follow along. I’ll share why we stopped using the usual tools, and what carries over to your APIs.</p>

### Sovereign Cloud-Native AI: Building Autonomous Enterprises Without Black-Box Dependencies

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Acacia 2
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Abhijit Dey

<p>As enterprises embrace Agentic AI, they risk replacing traditional cloud lock-in with a new form of dependency: AI lock-in. Critical decisions, data, reasoning, and automation increasingly reside in external models running in foreign clouds, exposing organisations to regulatory, operational, and geopolitical risks.</p>
<p>This session explores how Cloud-Native AI principles can be used to build sovereign AI ecosystems that are resilient, portable, auditable, and vendor-independent. Through practical architectures combining Kubernetes, open-weight models, agent frameworks, APIs, vector databases, and local inference platforms, we'll demonstrate how enterprises can retain full control of their data, models, and operations while enabling autonomous business processes at scale.</p>

### Sovereign Local-First E2EE solutions: ELFA workspace & NextGraph SDK

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Dragonnier 4
- Conference: Digital Resilience Europe
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Niko Bonnieure

<p>NextGraph is a protocol, a framework, and a platform that supports easy development of Local-First, decentralized, secure and private apps, with end-to-end encryption.</p>
<p>By combining the best of the local first world (CRDTs), a Graph database (RDF), DID (decentralized identifiers) for users and documents, and end-to-end encryption plus encryption at rest, we provide an SDK that offers all the requirements of portability, interoperability and security needed today for building credible alternatives to Big Tech platforms and products. The ecosystem of apps built with NextGraph is growing, specially with the <a href="https://elfaconsortium.eu">ELFA consortium</a> that is preparing a full suite of apps for productivity tools and social network: an integrated workspace for resilience and sovereignty.</p>

### Strongly Sandboxing AI Agents in the Millions

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Acacia 5
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Felipe Huici

### Who Decides What Goes on the Invoice? Billing an API From Its Contract

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: Agentic Engineering
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Alexandre Bergere

<p>Every API product with usage-based pricing ends up with two candidates for the truth: the contract the customer signed, and whatever the payment provider was told. When plans, usage and discounts live in the provider, the invoice is decided by a system that never saw the contract, and reconciliation becomes a monthly chore.</p>
<p>This talk shows the opposite split, as built in Kaiten, an open source control plane for B2B SaaS. The contract, the entitlements and the usage journal stay in one place, which rates every invoice line in integer minor units with a pure function that reads no clock, so a preview and the real invoice cannot disagree. Stripe receives finished lines and does what it does best: tax, numbering, sending and collection. It holds no product, price or subscription. Its read-back is compared at zero tolerance, and a mismatch voids and recomposes the invoice instead of correcting the source of truth from the provider. With no provider at all, invoices wait in a pull queue that an ERP claims and acknowledges, which works even air-gapped.</p>
<p>Attendees leave with a reference split for API monetization: which decisions belong to the contract and which to the payment provider, when to hold an invoice instead of sending it, and why a provider is never called inside a database transaction.</p>

### Your AI Agents Need Adult Supervision: Cloud-Native Patterns for Multi-Agent Systems

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Acacia 3
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Nadine Raiss

<p>Single-agent demos look great—until real users, real data, and real traffic show up. Then things get weird: loops, noisy tool calls, hidden costs, and “creative” agent behavior.</p>
<p>This talk is a practical guide to building multi-agent systems that behave in production. We’ll cover cloud-native patterns for agent orchestration, tool routing, state/memory boundaries, and failure isolation on Kubernetes-based platforms. You’ll see how to design workflows where agents collaborate without stepping on each other, and how to keep systems observable, testable, and debuggable.</p>
<p>We’ll also walk through what actually breaks first at scale—latency chains, prompt drift, brittle retries, and runaway automation—and how to fix it with architecture choices, not heroics.</p>
<p>If you’re moving from “agent prototype” to “agent platform,” this session gives you the engineering playbook to keep innovation fast while keeping operations sane.</p>

### Your API Has a New User. It Doesn’t Read the Docs, It Has 200,000 Tokens of Attention, and It Will Never Call Support.

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API for AI Governance
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Mehdi Yadir Goldfeil

<p>MCP is just an API,” they say. True, and that is exactly the problem. We hand AI agents APIs designed for humans, then act surprised when they hallucinate parameters, loop on 400 errors or call the wrong endpoint.</p>
<p>Your API has a new consumer. It never reads your docs, it runs on a fixed attention budget called a context window, and it will never open a support ticket. It simply fails, retries, or quietly works around you, just like the employee who goes back to their trusty spreadsheet.</p>
<p>The good news: we already know how learners succeed. This talk maps neuroscientist Stanislas Dehaene’s four pillars of learning (attention, active engagement, error feedback and consolidation) onto the design of APIs consumed by agents. You will see why a tool description is a prompt, why an error message is a lesson, why eighty CRUD endpoints are worth less than six intent-based operations, and how a consistent contract lowers cognitive cost for machines and humans alike.</p>
<p>Expect real before-and-after examples, a few lessons borrowed from military strategy, and a twelve-question Agent-Readiness checklist you can run on your own APIs on Monday morning. Because we no longer design APIs for readers. We design them for learners.</p>

### Your API changed, did your docs notice?

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 14:30–14:55
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Kim Rowden

<p>AI is dramatically increasing the speed at which software and APIs are built, changed, and shipped. Documentation, however, is still largely maintained through manual processes. That creates an increasingly important gap: the product evolves continuously, while the documentation describing it struggles to keep up. The deeper issue is that documentation is often treated as something that happens after development rather than as part of the development lifecycle itself.</p>
<p>As AI changes how software is built, we have an opportunity to rethink this model. Documentation can become aware of changes across code, tickets, specifications, and engineering context, identify what has become outdated, and propose updates while keeping humans in control. The result is documentation that evolves alongside the API rather than constantly chasing it.</p>

### Beyond Prototypes: Building Enterprise-scale Agentic Systems

- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit

- Speakers: Kevin Dubois

<p>There has been a lot of progress in agentic frameworks (such as Langchain, CrewAI, Autogen, among others), most of them written in and targeting Python developers. These frameworks allow you to build multi-agent systems that are co-located inside the same application.</p>
<p>This works well for fast experimentation and building prototypes, but production systems live or die on scalability, security, observability and governance. At the end of the day agents are just like any other type of enterprise software that we need to ship, run an operate on production.</p>
<p>In this session we will show how to build distributed agentic, MCP-connected systems that are ready for enterprise scale. We'll cover real production patterns such as agentic orchestration patterns, MCP interoperability, agent/tool coordination, observability and tracing and enterprise integration reusing our existing platforms.</p>

### From Concrete to Code : Bringing Modern APIs to the Energy Giants

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 4
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API for AI Governance
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Aya Lachbi

<p>The energy sector is historically built on concrete, steel, and decades of rigorous safety standards—not necessarily agile sprints or API-first design. For a long time, &quot;engineering&quot; meant building physical infrastructure. But as the energy transition accelerates, that definition is shifting rapidly.
In this session, I share my on-the-ground experience as an apprentice at Tractebel (ENGIE Group), working at the intersection of heavy industry and modern software architecture. This talk explores the unique challenge of deploying modern API strategies within a legacy industrial environment. I will discuss how we break down data silos between traditional engineering teams and IT, and the cultural friction that comes with replacing manual workflows with automated connectivity
Because the future of energy isn't just about atoms; it’s about bits. As traditional industries digitize, the ability to wrap complex physical systems in clean, consumable APIs is becoming a critical skill. Whether you are a senior architect or a fellow junior, you will gain a fresh perspective on how &quot;real-world&quot; industries are entering the API economy, and why the most impactful code today is often written where you least expect it.</p>

### GPU Sharing on Kubernetes: Running Inference Workloads Without Breaking the Bank

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Acacia 1
- Conference: Cloud Native AI Summit
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Jerome Baude, Skander Belli

<p>We don’t need to tell you that GPUs are the most expensive resource in your Kubernetes cluster (that’s what your cloud bill is for!). Yet, most teams still dedicate a singular GPU per workload and leave 70% of its compute capacity idle. That is money burning every hour. (A lot!)</p>
<p>And still most organizations don’t implement useful methods like time-slicing and MIG partitioning to get the most out of GPU workloads - so we will show you how!</p>
<p>In this talk we’ll talk through tried &amp; true practices alongside emerging methods for sharing GPUs across multiple inference workloads including: time-slicing, MIG partitioning, and the newer approaches in Kubernetes 1.30+. We will cover the trade-offs: latency impact of time-slicing, when MIG is better than time-slicing, how to configure the device plugin, and the scheduling changes you need.</p>
<p>You will see a live demo of running 4 LLM inference workloads on a single GPU with proper isolation, the monitoring setup that shows actual GPU utilization per workload, and the cost math that makes the case to your finance team. Real configs, real numbers, no theory.</p>

### Hallucinating in Three Dimensions: Lessons Learned from Trying Every 3D GenAI Tool

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 3
- Conference: Apidays

- Type: Talk

<p>When a text LLM hallucinates, it fabricates a fact. When a 3D model hallucinates, it breaks physics, inverts normals, and crashes pipelines. In immersive media, 3D animation, and game design, that's a real showstopper.</p>
<p>As studios rush to adopt generative AI across film, animation, XR, and virtual production, errors like impossible topology and asset inconsistencies are creeping into projects, often only discovered after hours have been wasted trying to integrate them into Unity or Unreal.</p>
<p>In this talk, I'll share lessons learned from experimenting with a wide range of 3D GenAI tools, what works, what doesn't, and some practical tips and best practices for using generative 3D media in creative pipelines.</p>

### The GNU Taler payment system

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Christian Grothoff

<p>GNU Taler is a Free Software privacy-friendly payment system from the GNU project.  Taler does not use a blockchain: monetary policies are expected to be set by a central bank.  Digital money can be issued either by retail banks or by the central bank. Paying with Taler is extremely efficient:
customers authorize transactions with one click, and then the system takes only a few milliseconds to finalize the transactions. In this lecture, we will introduce the political goals, architecture and technology behind Taler,
demonstrate the current state of the art and outline likely future developments.</p>

### The Non-Human Developer: Securing APIs for AI Agents in a Post-DORA World

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 2
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API and Context Management
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Harry Bouras

<p>For the past decade, we optimized API Developer Experience (DevEx) for humans. Today, our fastest-growing API consumers are autonomous AI agents. Unlike humans, agents are unpredictable, can trigger massive traffic spikes via &quot;hallucination loops,&quot; and consume millions of tokens. In highly regulated industries, an un-governed AI agent isn't just an IT headache under strict DORA enforcement, it is a severe compliance risk. This session explores the transition from DevEx to &quot;Agent Experience&quot; (AX) and how to architect Zero-Trust API gateways to protect enterprise core systems.</p>
<p>Key Takeaways &amp; Outline: To keep it engaging, I will anchor the talk in real-world metrics and architectural blueprints:</p>
<p>The Paradigm Shift: Why agentic workflows have officially surpassed human-driven API consumption.
The Chaos of the Machine: Quantifying the &quot;Hallucination Loop&quot; (e.g., how a confused AI agent can accidentally trigger 36k requests/hour) and massive LLM token costs.
The Regulatory Collision: The DORA reality, how an un-governed AI causing API downtime can lead to penalties up to 2% of global turnover.
Architecting the &quot;Agent-Proof&quot; API: Practical blueprints for semantic rate-limiting, dynamic PII masking at the edge, and intent-based verification.</p>

### The Ocean is a Product: Building Sovereign APIs for the Blue Economy

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:00–15:25
- Room: Aubépine 6
- Conference: Apidays
- Track: API Lifecycle and Architecture
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Cellou Diallo

<p>It is often said that we know more about the surface of the Moon than the depths of our own oceans. However, the real challenge today isn't just data collection—it's accessibility. Between satellite feeds, acoustic sensors, and subsea drones, the BlueTech sector is drowning in data, yet remains trapped in proprietary silos and 20th-century Excel files.
​In this session, we will explore how to apply the API-as-a-Product methodology to one of the most complex environments on Earth. I will share my 'battle scars' and insights from building Blue Connex, a sovereign API infrastructure designed to transform maritime 'Data Swamps' into seamless, secure, and interoperable services.
​We will discuss how to reconcile two often-conflicting imperatives: radical openness (for scientific innovation) and strict sovereignty (for the protection of strategic national resources). No hype—just practical lessons on designing interface contracts (OpenAPI) for the high seas, managing latency in offshore environments, and building trust-based gateways for the future of the Blue Economy.</p>

### RESI-STACK: Survivable Networking for a Resilient Europe

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:55–16:05
- Room: Dattier 3
- Conference: SUPERstack
- Type: Talk
- Speakers: Gioacchino Mazzurco

<p>Digital sovereignty means more than choosing where to host our services: it also means keeping communication useful when infrastructure fails. During disasters, communication is needed most—to coordinate emergency response, request help and reach loved ones—yet this is precisely when ordinary networks may become unavailable.
Drawing on my experience with LibreMesh, LibreRouter and RetroShare, I will introduce RESI-STACK, a proposed decentralized connectivity substrate within SUPERstack. Its goal is to help applications communicate over whatever links remain available—from Wi-Fi and Ethernet to LoRa and physically carried data—without depending on continuous Internet access.
The proposed design combines cryptographic peer identifiers, adaptive routing and delay-tolerant delivery. An intent-based API would let applications express their communication needs and adapt to degraded connectivity rather than simply fail.
Research and development will begin in January 2027. This talk presents the motivation, architectural direction and open research questions—not a finished implementation. It is an invitation to discuss trade-offs, identify real-world requirements and explore collaborations toward a more resilient European communications infrastructure.
This proposal is part of the SUPERstack track.</p>

- Date: 3 December 2026
- Time: 15:55–16:15
- Room: Davidia 1
- Conference: GreenIO
